It guards against a disagreement between this crate and `escher-signature` about the orders of the corners or the area of the orbifold. Section 15.3 asks the owner whether to keep it.

Check 4 evaluates the relator in the corner frame, where vertex $j$ is the origin. A power of a matrix is the identity exactly when the same power of its conjugate is, which Section 15.1 records as an assumption. The frame is a choice for accuracy. The test "Relators in the domain frame" of Section 12.1 computes, in the reference arithmetic, the residual of check 4 from the exact power of the `f64` pairings in each frame, the largest over the three corners:

| Triple | Domain frame | Corner frame | Tolerance of check 4 |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| $(998, 981, 961)$ | $5.426 \times 10^{-5}$ | $1.338 \times 10^{-7}$ | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ |
| $(1000, 3, 2)$ | $3.286 \times 10^{-12}$ | $8.746 \times 10^{-14}$ | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ |

At $(998, 981, 961)$, the triple with the largest residual of check 4 (Section 8.4), the domain frame fails the tolerance even with exact arithmetic, and the corner frame passes it. Check 4 has the smallest margin of the six. Repeated multiplication is chosen over repeated squaring, on figures that Section 14 records and no measurement of the code covers.

Check 4 alone does not fix the angle, because it also passes for an angle of $k\pi/n_j$. Check 5 closes that gap. Check 5 uses only the vertices, and checks 2, 3, and 4 tie the walls and the pairings to the vertices.

`verify()` costs at most $3 \cdot 999 + 18$ matrix products at the order limit. Each corner needs $n_j - 1$ products for the power, two for its frame, and three for its conjugate, and check 2 needs three more for the pairing relators.

For a Euclidean domain, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, and reads each one in this way. The vertex condition of the checks 2 to 6 holds for a Euclidean point, whose last coordinate is 1, and indexes are modulo $m$:

| Check | Euclidean reading | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | `euclidean::isometry_defect` of each pairing | [RAT], Theorem 1.3.5 (Section 12 of the geometry reference) |
| 2. Pairings | As for a hyperbolic domain, with `euclidean::side` | As for a hyperbolic domain |
| 3. Walls | For each side $j$, the largest of: the absolute value of `euclidean::side(covector_j, E)` divided by the last coordinate of $E$ for $E = V_{j-1}$ and $E = V_j$, the largest absolute entry of `pairing_j - euclidean::reflection(covector_j)`, the largest absolute coordinate of `normal_j - (c_1, c_2, 0)` for the covector $c$, $\lvert c_1^2 + c_2^2 - 1 \rvert$, and the convexity term with `euclidean::side` | A line covector of a line through the endpoints of its side, stored with its unit direction, whose reflection is the pairing (Sections 4.5 and 6.6 of the geometry design) |
| 4. Relators | For each corner $j$, $C = P_j P_{j+1}$, and $C^{n_j}$ by $n_j - 1$ successive multiplications. The residual is the largest absolute entry of $C^{n_j} - I$ divided by $n_j$, maximized over the corners. There is no corner frame | Item 4 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference, and the local group $\langle a, b : a^2 = b^2 = (ab)^n = 1 \rangle$ of a corner reflector of order $n$ of [TH], Proposition 13.3.1(iii), p. 308 (Section 9.4 of the notation reference) |
| 5. Angles | The largest $\lvert \theta_j - \pi/n_j \rvert$, with $\theta_j$ of Section 7. The condition on the angle sum of a hyperbolic triangle does not apply | Every corner has the angle $\pi/n_j$ (item 4 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference) |
| 6. Area | $\lvert (m - 2)\pi - \sum_j \theta_j \rvert$, against the value $-2\pi\chi = 0$ of a Euclidean signature | A consistency check only, as below |

Check 6 of a Euclidean domain has the residual 0 on every Euclidean domain of Section 9.4 (Section 8.4), and passes on the triangle of `*442` labelled `*632`, where checks 4 and 5 fail (Section 8.3). The design keeps it so that the six checks exist for every geometry, and Section 15.3 puts that to the owner.

For a spherical domain, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, and reads each one in this way, with indexes modulo $m$:

| Check | Spherical reading | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | `spherical::isometry_defect` of each pairing | [RAT], Theorem 2.1.3 (Section 11.1 of the geometry reference) |
| 2. Pairings | As for a hyperbolic domain, with `spherical::side`. The endpoint part is the largest absolute coordinate of $P_i E - E$, with no division by a last coordinate | As for a hyperbolic domain |
| 3. Walls | For each side $j$, the largest of: the absolute value of `spherical::side(covector_j, E)` for $E = V_{j-1}$ and $E = V_j$, with no division, the largest absolute entry of `pairing_j - spherical::reflection(normal_j)`, the largest absolute coordinate of `covector_j - normal_j`, $\lvert \text{normal}_j \cdot \text{normal}_j - 1 \rvert$, and the convexity term with `spherical::side` and no division. Also $\lvert V \cdot V - 1 \rvert$ for each vertex $V$, and, for a lune, which includes the domains of `nx`, `x`, and `*`, the largest absolute coordinate of $V_0 + V_1$ | A great-circle normal of a great circle through the endpoints of its side, stored as its own covector, whose reflection is the pairing (Sections 4.6 and 6.7 of the geometry design). Each vertex is a sphere point, and the two vertices of a lune are antipodal (Section 6.7) |
| 4. Relators | For each corner $j$, $C = P_j P_{j+1}$, and $C^{n_j}$ by $n_j - 1$ successive multiplications. The residual is the largest absolute entry of $C^{n_j} - I$ divided by $n_j$, maximized over the corners. There is no corner frame. For a lune this is $(P_0 P_1)^n$ at corner 0 and $(P_1 P_0)^n$ at corner 1 | Item 4 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference, and the local group of a corner reflector of [TH], Proposition 13.3.1(iii), p. 308 (Section 9.4 of the notation reference) |
| 5. Angles | The largest $\lvert \theta_j - \pi/n_j \rvert$, with $\theta_j$ of Section 7, or infinity when the angle sum of a triangle is not above $\pi$. Both angles of a lune come from its normals (DOM-20) | Every corner has the angle $\pi/n_j$ (item 4 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference), and the angle sum of a spherical triangle exceeds $\pi$ ([RAT], Theorem 2.5.1, p. 50) |
| 6. Area | The absolute difference between `area()` and the stored area of the signature, $\pi$ times `area_over_pi()`, which is $2\pi\chi$ | The areas of a triangle and of a lune of Section 7, and the area $2\pi\lvert\chi\rvert$ of an elliptic orbifold of [TH], p. 312 (Section 7.3 of the notation reference) |

Check 3 reads the vertices of a spherical domain because both sides of a lune have the side value 0 at every point of the second axis. Without the vertex terms, a lune whose vertices coincide or lie off the sphere would pass every check.

DOM-21. For a spherical domain, the precondition of checks 2 to 6 MUST require each vertex to be finite and not the zero vector, and MUST NOT require a positive last coordinate, because a vertex of a spherical domain may lie on or below the plane $x_3 = 0$, as both vertices of a lune do, and the precondition of the other geometries would give every lune an infinite residual.

`verify()` of a spherical domain costs no more products than the bound above. At the order limit, a lune needs $2 \cdot 999$ products for its two relators.

For a double, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, reads each one in its geometry as above, and changes them in this way:

| Check | Reading for a double | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | Unchanged | As above |
| 2. Pairings | With the structural term of DOM-27, and the endpoint terms and the wall term of a side that is not a mirror | Condition 3.2 (a) to (d) of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994], and the hypothesis of Theorem 12 of [SOLOMYAK2020] that no side is paired with itself (Sections 4.1 and 4.9 of the domain reference) |
| 3. Walls | No reflection term, because no side is a mirror. The covector term and the convexity term in the geometry of the domain, with no division for a spherical domain | A wall stored with its covector, and a convex polygon (`UNV-31`) |
| 4. Relators | The walk of the vertex cycles of DOM-28 | Definition 11 and Theorem 12 of [SOLOMYAK2020], and Conditions 3.10 and 3.13 and Lemma 3.12 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] (item 3 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference) |
| 5. Angles | The largest $\lvert \theta_k - c_k \pi / n_k \rvert$, with $\theta_k$ of Section 7, $n_k$ the order, and $c_k$ the copies of vertex $k$. For a hyperbolic kite, infinity when the angle sum of either triangle of DOM-29 is not below $\pi$, and for a spherical kite, when it is not above $\pi$ | The cycle condition of item 3 of Section 4.8 of the domain reference, read per vertex through its copies, and the angle sums of a triangle of Section 10.1 of the geometry reference and of [RAT], Theorem 2.5.1, p. 50 |
| 6. Area | The absolute difference between `area()` and the stored area of the rotation signature, and for a Euclidean double $\lvert (m - 2)\pi - \sum_k \theta_k \rvert$ | As for a reflection domain of its geometry |

A reflection domain reads check 5 in the same way, because each of its corners has the copies 1.

DOM-27. Check 2 MUST fail structurally unless the kind, the partner, the orientation, the pairing, and the geometry of every side agree: a side of the kind `Mirror` is its own partner and has the orientation `Reversing`, a side of the kind `GlideReflection` or `RotaryReflection` has a partner other than itself and the orientation `Reversing`, a side of the kind `GlideReflection` belongs to a domain that is not spherical and a side of the kind `RotaryReflection` to a spherical domain (DOM-61), a side of the kind `Rotation` or `Translation` has a partner other than itself and the orientation `Preserving`, and a side has the kind `Translation` exactly when its pairing passes the translation test. The translation test holds when the domain is Euclidean and each entry of the linear part of the pairing, its upper-left 2 by 2 block, is within $10^{-9}$, the tolerance of check 2, of the identity, or when the domain is hyperbolic and the determinant of the pairing is positive and its trace is above 3. It never holds for a spherical domain. A glide reflection never passes the translation test. In the Euclidean plane its linear part is a reflection and not the identity. In the hyperbolic plane its determinant is negative, so it is never a translation. The trace alone would not exclude it. The unit test `hyperbolic_glide_reflection_is_not_a_translation` measures the traces 1.2553, 3.000146, 6.5244, and 147.42 for glide reflections of the lengths 0.5, 1.317, 2, and 5, so a test of the trace alone would class the three longer ones as translations. A hyperbolic isometry that preserves orientation has the trace $1 + 2\cosh \ell$ when it is a translation by the length $\ell$, which is above 3, and a trace in $[-1, 3)$ when it is a rotation. Section 14 records this classification as unverified, with the smallest trace of a translation that the sweeps measured. The rule exists because the renderer reads the partner, the orientation, and the pairing of a side and never its kind, so a kind that these contradict would go undetected. `escher info` would then print a false kind in its `Side pairs` line.

DOM-61. Check 2 MUST treat a side of the kind `RotaryReflection` as it treats a side of the kind `GlideReflection`: a partner other than the side itself, the orientation `Reversing`, a pairing with a negative determinant, the endpoint term with the endpoints in the same order, $P_i V_{i-1} = V_{p-1}$ and $P_i V_i = V_p$, the wall term, and a pairing that never passes the translation test, which never holds on a spherical domain. It MUST also fail structurally for a side of the kind `RotaryReflection` on a domain that is not spherical, and for a side of the kind `GlideReflection` on a spherical domain. The reasons are that a rotary reflection of the lune of `nx` maps its side onto its partner with the endpoints in the same order, as the prototype of Section 14 measured, and that the kind is a label that `escher info` prints and the renderer never reads, so only the plane rule keeps a false label from passing: on the prototype, `x` with its pairings labelled `GlideReflection` passed `verify()` and enumerated to the order 2 without the plane rule. The plane rule is `DEC-57` of the architecture.

DOM-28. Check 4 of a domain with a side that is not a mirror MUST walk the vertex cycles by the state walk of DOM-55 and evaluate the relator of each cycle, and check 4 of a domain whose sides are all mirrors MUST evaluate the corner relators as item 4 of Section 8.2 states, because the corner relator $(P_j P_{j+1})^{n_j}$ holds between two mirrors, while a cycle that meets a paired side has the relator of its walk. A domain that mixes mirrors and paired sides walks its cycles like any other domain with a paired side.

DOM-55. The walk of the vertex cycles MUST run over the states $(v, d)$ of a vertex $v$ and a direction $d$ of $+$ or $-$, by the steps and the table below, with the structural limit of $2m$ steps for each cycle and the structural conditions below, because one walk then covers the doubles, the glide reflections, the mirrors beside paired sides, and the domains with only translations, and it reproduces the walk of the doubles step for step, so that every double keeps its cycles, its relator texts, and its residuals. From the state $(v, +)$ the next side is $s = (v + 1) \bmod m$, and from $(v, -)$ it is $s = v$. The walk applies $g \leftarrow P_s g$, and with $p$ the partner of $s$ it moves to the state of this table:

| $P_s$ | From $(v, +)$ | From $(v, -)$ |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Orientation `Preserving` | $(p, +)$ | $(p - 1, -)$ |
| Orientation `Reversing`, which includes a mirror with $p = s$ | $(p - 1, -)$ | $(p, +)$ |

Indexes are modulo $m$. The walk starts at $(v, +)$ for $v = 0, 1, \dots, m - 1$ in order, skips a start state that an earlier cycle visited, and repeats the step until the state is the start state again. It marks each visited state and its flip $(v, -d)$ as visited, because the reversed walk of a cycle gives the inverse relator. A walk that takes more than $2m$ steps fails structurally. For each cycle:

- Structural: every vertex of the cycle stores the same order, which is the cycle order $m_c$. Every domain without the closure `Cycles` of Section 3.2 also requires the copies of the distinct vertices of the cycle to sum to 2. These domains include every double of Section 6.8 and `2222` at every value of `skew`. The wallpaper domains, the spherical kites, and the domains of `nx` and `x` have the closure `Cycles` and need no such condition. A vertex that the walk visits in both directions adds its copies once.
- Numeric: the largest absolute coordinate of $g V_{\text{start}} - V_{\text{start}}$, divided by the last coordinate of $V_{\text{start}}$ for a hyperbolic or Euclidean domain. This is the condition that the cycle transformation fixes its vertex (Condition 3.10 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994]).
- Numeric: the largest absolute entry of $C^{m_c} - I$ divided by $m_c$, with $C^{m_c}$ by $m_c - 1$ successive multiplications. For a hyperbolic domain, $C = F^{-1} g F$ in the corner frame of $V_{\text{start}}$, with $F =$ `hyperbolic::translation(V_start)`, and otherwise $C = g$. A hyperbolic domain with five or more vertices evaluates $g$, both numeric parts, and the frame by DOM-49.

The residual is the largest numeric part over the cycles, or infinity when a structural condition fails.

For a domain whose pairings all preserve orientation, the walk stays in the states $(v, +)$ and is the walk of Definition 11 of [SOLOMYAK2020], step for step: $s = (v + 1) \bmod m$, $g \leftarrow P_s g$, and $v = $ `partner(s)`. The walk of a domain with a reversing pairing follows the cycle of Conditions 3.7, 3.10, and 3.13 and Lemma 3.12 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994], which passes to the other face at each step and puts no condition on the orientation of a pairing (Section 4.9 of the domain reference). A corner between two mirrors gives two states, and its angle $\theta$ counts twice, so the condition is $2\theta = 2\pi/m$, as Example 3.15 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] shows. The table of directions and the limit of $2m$ steps are this design's reading of those conditions for a polygon in the plane, which no source states in this form, and Section 14 records it with `UNV-32`.

On a prototype outside the repository, the state walk and the walk of Definition 11 gave the same cycles, the same relator texts, bit-identical residuals of check 4, and the same `Verification` on 647 doubles and on 500 values of `aspect` of `2222`. The doubles were those of $pqr$ with orders up to 12, the named spherical and Euclidean doubles, the doubles with orders of 1000, and the doubles of polygons (Section 14). On the ten domains of Section 6.13, it found the cycles and the orders of the table of DOM-50 at every setting of its grid of Section 8.4 and at each of its random settings, covered each of the $2m$ states exactly once with its flip, revisited no state, and terminated.

The condition on the copies stays for the doubles, because without it the prototype passed two defects that the unit tests `double_whose_cycle_copies_do_not_sum_to_two_gives_invalid_pairing` and `verify_fails_on_a_double_with_wrong_copies` inject. The all-mirror path of the corner relators (DOM-33) does not change, so the reflection domains keep their values bit for bit (DOM-30).

DOM-56. Check 5 of a wallpaper domain, of `2222` at every value of `skew`, and of a spherical group domain with the closure `Cycles`, which are the spherical kites and the domains of `nx` and `x`, MUST use the cycle rule: its residual is the largest, over the cycles of DOM-55, of the absolute difference between the angle sum over the states of the cycle and $2\pi / m_c$, where a state $(v, d)$ adds the angle $\theta_v$ of Section 7 of its vertex. Check 5 of every other domain MUST be per vertex, with the copies. The reasons are that the angle at a vertex of a sheared `2222` or of `o` is not a fixed fraction of $\pi$, so no per-vertex rule holds there, while the angle sum of each cycle is $2\pi / m_c$ by Lemma 3.12 and Condition 3.13 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994], and that the other domains keep their residuals of check 5 bit for bit. When the walk of a domain fails structurally, the residual of check 5 is infinity. A spherical kite needs the cycle rule as a wallpaper kite does, because the vertices of its chain have the order 1 and the right angle, which no per-vertex rule with the copies 1 accepts: on the prototype, the spherical kites with the closure `PerVertex` failed with an infinite residual of check 4 and a residual of 1.57 of check 5. A spherical kite keeps the condition of the table of the doubles, that check 5 is infinity when the angle sum of either of its two triangles is not above $\pi$.

On the prototype, the cycle rule and the per-vertex rule gave bit-identical residuals of check 5, all 0, for `2222` at `skew` 0 over 506 values of `aspect`. This was a separate run from the comparison of the walks above, which used 500 values of `aspect`. This design chooses the cycle rule for `2222` at every value of `skew`, because the sheared domain needs it and one rule for one signature keeps the check uniform over its parameters. The crate MUST confirm the equality at `skew` 0 with the test "Cycle rule of `2222`" (Section 12.1). A chain counts each vertex that it visits in both directions twice: the chain $\{V_0, V_1\}$ of `**` has the states $(0, +)$, $(0, -)$, $(1, -)$, and $(1, +)$ and the angle sum $4 \cdot \pi/2 = 2\pi$. The expected cycles are those of the table of Section 6.8. For a kite, the cycles $\{W_0\}$ and $\{W_2\}$ are the rotations about corners 0 and 1 of the generating triangle, and the cycle $\{W_1, W_3\}$ is a product of four reflections.

At a flat vertex of a Euclidean double, check 5 compares an arccosine of an argument close to $-1$ with $\pi$, where the arccosine is ill-conditioned. A small tilt of a wall can therefore pass check 5, so checks 2 and 3 carry that case through the vertices and the pairings. A kite measures its flat vertex as two right angles (DOM-29), and the double of `22` measures its angle with `atan2` (DOM-20), so this does not apply to either.

A wallpaper domain, and `2222` with `skew` other than 0, measure a flat vertex by the same arccosine. On the prototype the argument there was exactly $-1$ at every setting of its grid of Section 8.4 and at each of its random settings, so the angle was exactly $\pi$ (Section 6.13), and checks 2 and 3 still carry a tilted wall.

For one of the ten domains of Section 6.13, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, reads each one as for a Euclidean domain, and changes them in this way:

| Check | Reading for one of the ten domains | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | Unchanged | As above |
| 2. Pairings | The structural term of DOM-27, the endpoint term of each side by its kind (exchanged for `Rotation` and `Translation`, in order for `GlideReflection` and `Mirror`), and the wall term of every side that is not a mirror | Condition 3.2 (a) to (d) of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] |
| 3. Walls | The reflection term for the sides of the kind `Mirror` only, and 0 for every other side, with the other terms of a Euclidean domain | As for a Euclidean domain |
| 4. Relators | The state walk of DOM-55, with no condition on the copies except for `2222` | Conditions 3.7, 3.10, and 3.13 and Lemma 3.12 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] |
| 5. Angles | The cycle rule of DOM-56 | Lemma 3.12 and Condition 3.13 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] |
| 6. Area | $\lvert (m - 2)\pi - \sum_k \theta_k \rvert$, as for every Euclidean domain | A consistency check only |

For a spherical group domain, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, reads each one as for a spherical domain, and changes them in this way:

| Check | Reading for a spherical group domain | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | Unchanged | As above |
| 2. Pairings | The structural term of DOM-27 with the plane rule of DOM-61, the endpoint term of each side by its kind (exchanged for `Rotation`, in order for `RotaryReflection` and `Mirror`), and the wall term of every side that is not a mirror | Condition 3.2 (a) to (d) of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] |
| 3. Walls | The reflection term for the sides of the kind `Mirror` only, the other terms of a spherical domain, and the term $V_0 + V_1$ for the domains of `nx`, `x`, and `*` | As for a spherical domain |
| 4. Relators | The state walk of DOM-55, with no condition on the copies, for the domains with a paired side, and the corner relators of DOM-28 for `*` | Conditions 3.7, 3.10, and 3.13 and Lemma 3.12 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994], and the local group of a corner reflector |
| 5. Angles | The cycle rule of DOM-56 for the domains with a paired side, with the condition on the triangles of a spherical kite, and per vertex for `*` | Lemma 3.12 and Condition 3.13 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] |
| 6. Area | The absolute difference between `area()` and the stored area of the signature, as for every spherical domain | A consistency check only |

The tolerances stay as DOM-9 states. On the crate, every spherical group domain in the range of Section 4 passes every check with a smallest margin of $3.919 \times 10^{3}$ (Section 8.4).

DOM-62. `verify()` MUST give a spherical domain with no side, no vertex, and no corner the residual 0 in checks 2 to 5, whose conditions hold vacuously, and MUST compute check 6 as for every spherical domain, from `area()` of $4\pi$ (Section 7), while a hyperbolic or Euclidean domain with no side MUST keep the infinite residuals of checks 2 to 6. `side_presentation()` MUST give it the presentation with no generator and no relator of Section 5.4. The reason is that the whole sphere of `1` is the one domain without a side that `build_with` returns, while a domain of another geometry without a side can come only from a defect. These are the two changes that the prototype of Section 14 found necessary and sufficient: the precondition `vertices_are_points` of `src/verify.rs` accepts no vertex for a spherical domain with no side, and `area()` returns $4\pi$ for a spherical domain with no vertex. Without the two changes, the prototype reported the residual 0 for check 1, infinity for checks 2 to 6, and the value NaN of `area()`. With the first change alone, its report says "the area is still inf" and does not say whether that is the value of `area()` or the residual of check 6. A NaN value of `area()` gives check 6 an infinite residual, because a residual that is not finite is reported as infinity (Section 8.2), so the report may refer to the residual of check 6, which this design does not confirm. With both changes, every residual was 0, a wrong stored area failed check 6, and the domains of `*732` and `*442`, emptied in the same way, still failed five checks. The report of the prototype does not say whether it removed their sides with their vertices. The row "Every vertex removed" of the test "Failing domains" keeps the sides of `*732`, so the changes do not affect it. This is the domain of `DEC-58` of the architecture.

The walk needs one product for each step of each cycle, at most $2m$ in all, which is $m$ for a domain whose pairings all preserve orientation. With the frames, the powers, and check 2, the cost of `verify()` of a double at the order limit stays about 3000 matrix products, as for a reflection domain, because the powers dominate and a kite has three cycles.

For a polygon and for the double of a polygon, `verify()` computes the same six checks with the same tolerances, as for a hyperbolic triangle and a kite, with these readings:

| Check | Reading for a polygon or its double | Condition |
| --- | --- | --- |
| 1 to 4 | Unchanged. Check 4 evaluates a corner relator at each of the $n$ corners of a polygon, and walks the vertex cycles of the table of Section 6.8 for a double | As above |
| 5. Angles | The largest $\lvert \theta_k - c_k \pi / n_k \rvert$, with $\theta_k$ of DOM-46. For four vertices, infinity when the angle sum of either triangle of DOM-29 is not below $\pi$. For five or more vertices, infinity when $\sum_k \theta_k$ is not below $(m - 2)\pi$ | Every corner has the angle $\pi/n_j$, and the angle sum of a hyperbolic polygon is below $(m - 2)\pi$ (Theorem 7.16.2 of [BEA], Section 10.1 of the geometry reference) |
| 6. Area | The absolute difference between `area()` and the stored area of the signature, with `area()` of DOM-46 | The area $(m - 2)\pi - \sum_k \theta_k$ of Theorem 7.15.1 of [BEA], and the area $-2\pi\chi$ of a hyperbolic orbifold |

For five or more vertices, the residual of check 6 is $\lvert \sum_k (\theta_k - c_k \pi / n_k) \rvert$, the sum of the angle errors, which is at most $m$ times the residual of check 5 and has the same tolerance. It adds no independent information about the polygon. On a prototype outside the repository, check 6 was the check that failed first in 380 of 391 failing cases with five corners and 115 of 117 with eight, and in most failures at the default shape (Section 14). The design keeps its tolerance of $10^{-9}$, so check 6 binds the guard, and item 4 of Section 15.3 puts the choice to the owner.

DOM-49. Check 4 of a hyperbolic domain with five or more vertices MUST be evaluated in the double-double arithmetic of Section 6.10, from the stored `f64` values: the corner relator $P_j P_{j+1}$ and the cycle transformation $g$ as products of the stored pairings, the fixed-point term $g V - V$, the corner frame as the translation in closed form of step 8 of Section 6.10 of the vertex normalized in double-double arithmetic, with the inverse that negates the entries that mix the time coordinate with a space coordinate, and the power by $m_c - 1$ successive multiplications. Each entry of a residual MUST be rounded to `f64` once, before the division by the order, and the tolerance stays $2 \times 10^{-6}$. Check 4 of a domain with three or four vertices, and of every Euclidean and spherical domain, MUST stay in `f64` arithmetic as above. The reason is that in `f64`, the product of two pairings of a double with entries of about $10^{2}$ to $10^{4}$, for walls far from the centre, cancels to a rotation with much smaller entries, and the corner frame of a far vertex and the power then amplify the rounding of that product. Evaluated in `f64`, check 4 fails hundreds of doubles with ordinary orders, such as `99999999`, through that rounding alone, and its outcome near the tolerance depends on it (Section 8.4). The path in `f64` keeps every value and residual of the triangles, the kites, the rectangle, the lune, and the polygons with four corners bit for bit (DOM-30, Section 8.4). The corner relators of the starred polygons with five or more corners go through the same evaluation.

`verify()` of a polygon costs at most $m \cdot 999 + 6m$ matrix products at the order limit, which is 8040 for eight corners, because each corner needs $n_j - 1$ products for its power and five for its frame and its conjugate, and check 2 needs one for each side. The double of a polygon of eight corners has 14 sides and eight vertex cycles, and costs about as much. With five or more vertices, each product of check 4 is a double-double product (DOM-49), which costs several times an `f64` product, so `verify()` of a polygon or a double with an order of 1000 takes about a millisecond in a release build. Section 9.7 states the times.

### 8.3. Defects Each Check Detects

The test "Failing domains" of Section 12.1 injects each defect of this table into a domain built through private access, and asserts that exactly the checks of its row fail and that `passed()` is false. A defect without named triangles is applied to the domains of $(7, 3, 2)$, $(4, 4, 4)$, and $(1000, 3, 2)$. A tilt is a rotation of the normal about the origin by `hyperbolic::rotation`:

| Defect | Checks that fail |
| --- | --- |
| The pairing of side 0 scaled by $1 + 10^{-6}$ | 1, 2, and 3 |
| The partner of side 0 set to side 1 | 2 |
| The partner of side 2 set to 3, outside the range of the sides | 2 |
| Sides 0 and 1 swapped | 2, 3, and 4, and at $(4, 4, 4)$ only 2 and 3 |
| The normal of side 0 tilted by $10^{-6}$, with its covector and pairing computed from the tilted normal | 2, 3, and 4 |
| The normal and covector of side 0 tilted by $10^{-3}$, with the pairing left as constructed | 3 |
| The triangle $(7, 3, 3)$ labelled `*732` | 4, 5, and 6 |
| The triangle $(1001, 3, 2)$ labelled `*(1000)32` | 4, 5, and 6 |
| The triangle with the order of one corner raised by 1, labelled with the orders $(7, 3, 2)$ or $(4, 4, 4)$, for each of the three corners, such as $(7, 4, 2)$ labelled `*732` | 4, 5, and 6 |
| The triangle $(6, 4, 3)$ labelled with the orders $(6, 3, 2)$ of `*632` and its Euclidean area 0 | 4, 5, and 6 |
| Vertex 0 negated, which puts it on the other sheet of the hyperboloid | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| A NaN in the first coordinate of vertex 1 | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| A NaN in the normal of side 1 | 3, with an infinite residual |
| A NaN in one entry of the pairing of side 2 | 1, 2, 3, and 4, each with an infinite residual |
| The last vertex removed, on $(7, 3, 2)$ only | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| Every vertex removed, on $(7, 3, 2)$ only | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |

The test also injects these defects into Euclidean domains, which pass every check before the defect: the relabelled triangle is that of `*442`, and the other defects are applied to the domain of `*632` and to the rectangle of `*2222` at `aspect` 1. On each domain of its row, exactly the checks of the row fail:

| Defect | Checks that fail |
| --- | --- |
| The triangle of `*442` labelled with the orders of `*632` | 4 and 5. Check 6 passes |
| The offset $t$ of the covector of side 0 moved by $10^{-6}$, with the pairing left as constructed | 3 |
| The offset $t$ of side 0 moved by $10^{-6}$, with the pairing computed from the moved covector | 2 and 3 |
| The pairing of side 0 scaled by $1 + 10^{-6}$ | 1, 2, and 3. Check 4 passes |
| The partner of side 0 set to side 1 | 2 |
| A NaN in the first coordinate of vertex 1 | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| The last row of the pairing of side 0 set to $(10^{-6}, 0, 1)$ | 1, 2, and 3 |
| The rectangle of `*2222` labelled with the three orders $(6, 3, 2)$, on the rectangle only | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| The last vertex removed | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |
| Every vertex removed | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |

The test also injects these defects into spherical domains, which pass every check before the defect: the domains of `*532`, `*722`, and `*55`, unless the row names others. A tilt is a rotation of the normal about the origin by `spherical::rotation`. On each domain of its row, exactly the checks of the row fail:

| Defect | `*532` | `*722` | `*55` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The normal of side 0 tilted by $10^{-7}$, with its covector and pairing computed from the tilted normal | 2 and 3 | 2 and 3 | 2 and 3 |
| The pairing of side 0 scaled by $1 + 10^{-6}$ | 1, 2, and 3 | 1, 2, and 3 | 1, 2, and 3 |
| The triangle of `*432` labelled with the orders and the area $\pi/30$ of `*532` | 4, 5, and 6 | Not applied | Not applied |
| The lune of the angle $2\pi/n$ labelled with the order $n$, for $n = 5$ and $n = 12$ | Not applied | Not applied | 5 and 6, for both orders |
| The vertices of the lune set to $(0, 1, 0)$ and $(0, 1, 0)$, which coincide, or to $(0, 0.5, 0)$ and $(0, -3, 0)$, which lie off the sphere | Not applied | Not applied | 3, for both pairs |
| The partner of side 0 set to side 1 | 2 | 2 | 2 |
| The centre set to its negation, outside the domain | 2 | 2 | 2 |
| Vertex 0 set to the zero vector | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |

Check 4 passes on several of the spherical defects. No committed test prints the residuals below, which were printed once by hand:

- On the relabelled lunes, check 4 passes with the residuals $1.8 \times 10^{-16}$ and $3.2 \times 10^{-16}$.
- On the scaled pairing, check 4 passes with $1.000002 \times 10^{-6}$ at `*532`, $1.000003 \times 10^{-6}$ at `*722`, and $1.000002 \times 10^{-6}$ at `*55`.
- On the tilted wall, checks 2 and 3 fail with the residuals $7.064 \times 10^{-8}$ and $3.560 \times 10^{-8}$ at `*532`, $1.699 \times 10^{-7}$ and $8.675 \times 10^{-8}$ at `*722`, and $1.809 \times 10^{-7}$ and $9.511 \times 10^{-8}$ at `*55`, each at least 35 times the tolerance of $10^{-9}$. Check 4 passes with $1.901 \times 10^{-7}$ at `*532` and $1.505 \times 10^{-7}$ at `*722`, 10.5 and 13.3 times below its tolerance of $2 \times 10^{-6}$, and with $1.5 \times 10^{-14}$ at `*55`.

In the hyperbolic table, check 4 passes on the scaled pairing at all three triangles, below its tolerance of $2 \times 10^{-6}$, and checks 1, 2, and 3 catch that defect. The residual of check 4 is $1.000003 \times 10^{-6}$ at $(7, 3, 2)$, a figure that no committed test prints.

Check 3 exists for the row with the pairing left as constructed. The renderer tests a point with the covector and moves it with the pairing, so a domain whose covector and pairing disagree corrupts the image while the other five checks pass. The tilted wall does not pass through the endpoints of its side, so the first two values of check 3 exceed its tolerance.

The test also injects these defects into doubles, which pass every check before the defect: the kites of `732`, `442`, `532`, and `722`, the double of the rectangle of `2222` at `aspect` 1, and the doubles of the lunes of `44` and `55`, as each row names. On each domain of its row, exactly the checks of the row fail:

| Defect | Domains | Checks that fail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The partner of side 0 set to side 0 itself | The kites | 2 and 4, each with an infinite residual |
| The kind of side 0 set to `Mirror`, with its pairing left as constructed | The kites | 2 and 3. Check 4 walks the cycles of the mixed domain by DOM-55 and passes |
| The pairing of side 0 replaced by its inverse | The kites | 2 and 4 |
| Side 1 given the normal and the covector of side 0 of the generating domain, as if the image were not taken | The kites | 2, with an infinite residual, and 3. Check 2 fails structurally, because the pairing of side 0 then moves the centre to the wrong side of the wall of its partner |
| A kite labelled with one order off by one: the kites of $(8, 3, 2)$, $(7, 4, 2)$, and $(7, 3, 3)$ labelled `732`, and the kites of $(4, 3, 2)$ and $(5, 2, 2)$ labelled `532` | The kites of these orders | 4, 5, and 6 |
| The copies of $W_0$ set to 1 | The kites and the double of `55` | 4 with an infinite residual, and 5 |
| The two halves of a split side merged into one side that is its own partner, with the kind `Rotation` | `722` and `2222` | 2 |
| The translation of sides 2 and 5 replaced by the half-turn about the midpoint of side 2, with the kind `Translation` kept | `2222` | 2 and 4, with no residual asserted |
| Both pairings replaced by the half-turn $\operatorname{diag}(-1, 1, -1)$ about the axis through the two vertices | `44` | 2 |
| The pairings of sides 0 and 1 replaced by the half-turn about $W_0$ | `732` | 2 and 4 |
| The kinds of sides 2 and 5 set to `Rotation` on `2222`, and of sides 0 and 1 set to `Translation` on `442` | `2222` at `aspect` 1 and `442` | 2, with an infinite residual |
| Vertex $W_3$ moved to $W_4 - (0.5, 0, 0)$, along the line of sides 3 and 4 and 0.5 outside the wall of side 5, which is not adjacent to it | `2222` at `aspect` 1 | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 |
| A NaN in the first coordinate of vertex 1 | Every double of this paragraph | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6, each with an infinite residual |

The rows of the merged halves, of the half-turn in place of the translation, and of the relabelled kinds also run on the sheared `2222` at `aspect` 1 and `skew` 0.25, with the same checks failing.

The test also injects these defects into polygons, which pass every check before the defect. The polygons are the regular polygons of `*33333`, `*22222`, and `*444444`, unless the row names others. The unit tests build them through private access with the helper `regular`, which computes the vertices of equation 24 of [HT] in `f64`, and put the walls through those vertices, as Section 12.1 describes. The measurement tests of Section 9.7 use another helper, which rounds reference vertices once. The rows use polygons with five or more corners, because a polygon with four corners, such as `*3333`, is measured as two triangles, as a kite is (DOM-46). On each polygon of its row, exactly the checks of the row fail. The unit tests print the residuals:

| Defect | Polygons | Checks that fail | Residuals of the failed checks, in the order of the checks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Two adjacent corners exchanged: the polygon of `*5432` labelled with the orders $(4, 5, 3, 2)$, with the same area | `*5432` at its default shape, as `build_with` returns it | 4 and 5. Check 6 passes, at $4.44 \times 10^{-16}$, because the angle sum is unchanged | 0.2378 and 0.1571 |
| The order of each corner raised by 1, one corner at a time, with the area of the new orders | `*33333` and `*444444` | 4, 5, and 6 | `*33333`: 0.375, 0.2618, and 0.2618. `*444444`: 0.200, 0.1571, and 0.1571 |
| Vertex 2 moved toward the centre by $10^{-3}$ along the geodesic, with the walls and pairings left as constructed | `*33333`, `*22222`, and `*444444` | 2, 3, 5, and 6. Check 4 passes, because it reads the pairings and only the frame of the vertex | `*33333`: $6.19 \times 10^{-4}$, $2.10 \times 10^{-4}$, $1.03 \times 10^{-3}$, and $7.86 \times 10^{-4}$. `*22222`: $1.24 \times 10^{-3}$, $5.14 \times 10^{-4}$, $1.80 \times 10^{-3}$, and $6.87 \times 10^{-4}$. `*444444`: $3.39 \times 10^{-4}$, $9.16 \times 10^{-5}$, $7.70 \times 10^{-4}$, and $6.87 \times 10^{-4}$ |
| The last vertex removed | `*33333`, `*22222`, and `*444444` | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 | Each infinite |
| A NaN in the first coordinate of vertex 1 | `*33333`, `*22222`, and `*444444` | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 | Each infinite |

The unit tests also run the other rows on the polygons that `build_with` returns for `*5432`, `*3333`, and `*22222` at their default shapes, which pass every check before the defect. On each of them, exactly the checks of the row fail. `*3333` has four corners and is measured as two triangles (DOM-46). The unit tests print these residuals:

| Defect | Polygon | Checks that fail | Residuals of the failed checks, in the order of the checks |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| The order of each corner raised by 1, one corner at a time, with the area of the new orders | `*5432` | 4, 5, and 6 | Corner 0: 0.1585, 0.1047, and 0.1047. Corner 1: 0.2000, 0.1571, and 0.1571. Corner 2: 0.3750, 0.2618, and 0.2618. Corner 3: 0.6667, 0.5236, and 0.5236 |
| The same | `*3333` | 4, 5, and 6 | Each corner: 0.3750, 0.2618, and 0.2618 |
| The same | `*22222` | 4, 5, and 6 | Each corner: 0.6667, 0.5236, and 0.5236 |
| Vertex 2 moved toward the centre by $10^{-3}$ along the geodesic, with the walls and pairings left as constructed | `*5432` | 2, 3, 5, and 6 | $6.34 \times 10^{-4}$, $2.77 \times 10^{-4}$, $1.08 \times 10^{-3}$, and $6.87 \times 10^{-4}$ |
| The same | `*3333` | 2, 3, 5, and 6 | $5.00 \times 10^{-4}$, $2.89 \times 10^{-4}$, $1.06 \times 10^{-3}$, and $7.07 \times 10^{-4}$ |
| The same | `*22222` | 2, 3, 5, and 6 | $1.24 \times 10^{-3}$, $5.14 \times 10^{-4}$, $1.80 \times 10^{-3}$, and $6.87 \times 10^{-4}$ |
| The last vertex removed | `*5432`, `*3333`, and `*22222` | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 | Each infinite, and none NaN |
| A NaN in the first coordinate of vertex 1 | `*5432`, `*3333`, and `*22222` | 2, 3, 4, 5, and 6 | Each infinite, and none NaN |

The test also injects these defects into the wallpaper domains, which pass every check before the defect. On a prototype outside the repository, with the real `verify()` and the state walk, exactly the checks of each row failed (Section 14). The test asserts the same rows, with the defect applied through private access:

| Defect | Domain | Checks that fail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The glides of sides 0 and 2 labelled `Translation` | `xx` | 2 |
| The glide of side 0 replaced by the translation with the same vector, so that its endpoints are exchanged | `xx` | 2 and 4 |
| The glides of sides 0 and 2 given the orientation `Preserving` | `xx` | 2 and 4 |
| Mirror side 1 labelled `GlideReflection` | `*x` | 2 |
| Mirror side 1 given the orientation `Preserving` | `*x` | 2 and 5 |
| The order of the cycle of vertex 2 set to 2 | `4*2` | 4 and 5 |
| The order of the corner at vertex 0 set to 4 | `4*2` | 5 |
| The order of the chain of vertices 1 and 3 set to 2 | `4*2` | 5 |
| The order of vertex 1 alone set to 2 | `3*3` | 4 and 5, and `side_presentation()` gives `InvalidPairing` |
| Sides 0 and 1 merged into one side that is its own partner, with the kind `Rotation` | `22*` | 2 |
| The pairing of side 0 replaced by the half-turn about $V_3$ | `22*` | 2 and 4 |
| The partners set to 0 and 3 and 1 and 2 | `o` | 2, 4, and 5 |
| The order of both vertices of one cycle set to 1 | `22x` | 4 and 5 |
| The order of both vertices of one cycle set to 4 | `22x` | 5 |

The report of the prototype names each defect in a few words. It states neither the setting nor, for `22x`, which cycle. The test applies each defect at `aspect` 1 and to the cycle $\{V_0, V_2\}$ of `22x`, and asserts the rows of this table. It runs the rows of `xx`, `*x`, `22x`, `22*`, and `o` once each on the domains that `build_with` returns, with `o` at its default `skew` of 0. The unit test "Wallpaper defects" runs the four rows of `4*2` and `3*3` once each on the built kites and sets each order through private access. `side_presentation()` succeeded on most of these defects, as it does on the defects of the doubles, so the guard of DOM-54 comes from `verify()` and not from the presentation.

The test also injects these defects into the spherical group domains, which pass every check before the defect, with the defect applied through private access. The first three rows and the last row come from the prototype of Section 14, with the real `verify()`. The fourth row follows from the plane rule of DOM-61, which the prototype did not implement for that row. The fifth to seventh rows come from the plan of the tests and not from the prototype: check 2 fails on each by DOM-27 and DOM-61, and on the crate no other check fails. The row of the Euclidean domains labelled `RotaryReflection` is the other half of the plane rule:

| Defect | Domain | Checks that fail |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The lune of `nx` paired by the rotation through $2\pi/n$ | `3x` and `4x` | 2 and 5 |
| The kite of `n22` with sides 0 and 1 as the mirrors, the wrong pair, labelled with the corners $[n, 1, 1, 1]$ of `High` | `3*` | 4 and 5 |
| The kite of `332` with sides 0 and 1 as mirrors, labelled `3*2` | `3*2` | 4, 5, and 6 |
| The pairings of `x` labelled `GlideReflection` | `x` | 2, through the plane rule of DOM-61 |
| The pairing of side 0 labelled `Rotation`, with the orientation `Reversing` kept | `3x` | 2, with an infinite residual |
| The pairings of sides 0 and 1 labelled `GlideReflection` | `3x` | 2, with an infinite residual |
| The pairing of side 0 replaced by its reflection $R_0$, which fixes both endpoints, with the kind `RotaryReflection` kept | `3x` | 2 alone, with an infinite residual. The walk of `nx` applies side 1 twice, so check 4 never reads side 0 |
| The glide reflections labelled `RotaryReflection`: sides 0 and 2 of `xx`, and all four sides of `22x` | `xx` and `22x` | 2 alone, with an infinite residual, through the plane rule of DOM-61. The test first asserts that each of these sides is a glide reflection |
| Vertex $W_2$ moved by $10^{-7}$ along the equator | `3*` | None. The position of $W_2$ is a choice that `verify()` does not check (Section 6.16) |

The prototype did not record at which $n$ it built the defect of `nx` with the rotation, so the test applies it at $n = 3$ and $n = 4$, one odd and one even order. The second row is the defect of the plan with the mirrors on the wrong pair under the stored corners of `n*`. Each of the first three rows is caught by `verify()` alone, so none of them exercises the census of DOM-64. The defects of Section 12.1 that only the census catches pass `verify()` by design. The row of the moved vertex is a negative result that the test asserts, so that a change that makes `verify()` read the position of $W_2$ is seen.

The unit test `verify_fails_on_a_spherical_kite_with_the_mirrors_of_another_group` runs the second and third rows. It builds each wrong kite by hand with the test helper `with_low_mirrors`, which turns sides 0 and 1 of a built double into mirrors with `Side::spherical_mirror` of their own normals. It does not use `on_kite`, which refuses the low pair on a double whose corner 0 has an odd order. For the second row, it gives the built `3*` the sides of the double of `322` with low mirrors. For the third, it gives the double of `332` with low mirrors the corners and the geometry of the built `3*2` and the closure `Cycles`. Each fails exactly the checks of its row: 4 and 5 for `3*`, and 4, 5, and 6 for `332` labelled `3*2`. The unit test `moved_flat_vertex_of_a_cone_mirror_kite_passes` runs the last row.

Copying the reflection of side 3 of `3*` onto side 2 is not a defect, because the two mirror sides of `n*` lie on one great circle (Section 6.16) and the two reflections differ only by rounding. The domain with this change passes its checks. So the guard test uses other defects of the kites (Section 12.1).

No order of `532` can be raised by one, because $(6, 3, 2)$, $(5, 4, 2)$, and $(5, 3, 3)$ are not spherical, so the row of an order off by one lowers the orders of `532` where a spherical triangle exists. No such kite changes the order of corner 2 of `532`.

The row of `44` tests the wall term of check 2. The half-turn fixes both vertices, as the endpoint terms require of a pairing of that double, so only the wall term fails, and without it the domain passes all six checks. The unit tests print the residual of check 2: 1.307 on `44`, and 1.597 on `732`. The row of the half-turn on `2222` keeps the kind `Translation` on a pairing that is not a translation, which the structural test of DOM-27 covers, and its test asserts which checks fail and no residual. The row of the relabelled kinds tests the translation test of DOM-27.

The row of the moved vertex tests the convexity term of check 3. $W_3$ stays on the line of sides 3 and 4, so the endpoint terms of check 3 stay 0, and check 3 fails only through its convexity term. The unit test prints the residuals: 0 for check 1, which passes, and 3, 0.5, 3, $\pi$, and $\pi$ for checks 2 to 6. It asserts that each check clears its tolerance by at least a factor of 8, on the side of a pass or of a failure.

The tolerances separate an order from its neighbour at the limit. Two measurements outside the repository, which no test repeats, give these figures. At $(1001, 3, 2)$ labelled `*(1000)32`, the residual of check 4 is $6.277 \times 10^{-6}$, which is 3.14 times its tolerance, and the residuals of checks 5 and 6 are each $3.138 \times 10^{-6}$. The second measurement covers every triangle near the limit with one corner order off by one:

- Labels: every hyperbolic $(n_0, n_1, n_2)$ with $n_0$ from 900 to 1000, $n_0 \ge n_1 \ge n_2$, and $n_1$ and $n_2$ from the set $\{2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7, 8, 10, 12, 20, 30, 50, 100, 200, 300, 500, 700, 900, 950, 970, 980, 990, 995, 998, 999, 1000\}$.
- Triangles: for each label, the triangle with one of its orders raised by 1, which gives 19,728 cases for each corner.

Every case fails checks 4, 5, and 6:

| Raised order | Smallest residual of check 4 over its tolerance | Where | Smallest residual of check 5 or 6 over its tolerance |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Corner 0 | 3.094 | $(999, 970, 950)$ labelled `*(998)(970)(950)` | 3138.5 |
| Corner 1 | 3.110 | $(1000, 999, 950)$ labelled `*(1000)(998)(950)` | 3138.5 |
| Corner 2 | 3.138 | $(1000, 1000, 1001)$ labelled `*(1000)(1000)(1000)` | 3138.5 |

### 8.4. Tolerances

DOM-9. Each check MUST use the tolerance of this table, because `escher info --check` prints the tolerance with a failed check, and the margins below are what make a pass meaningful at every supported order.

| Check | Tolerance | Largest residual | Where | Margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | $10^{-9}$ | $1.819 \times 10^{-11}$ | $(868, 4, 4)$ | 55.0 |
| 2. Pairings | $10^{-9}$ | $1.819 \times 10^{-11}$ | $(868, 4, 4)$ | 55.0 |
| 3. Walls | $10^{-9}$ | $5.203 \times 10^{-15}$ | $(923, 6, 5)$ | $1.92 \times 10^{5}$ |
| 4. Relators | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $1.338 \times 10^{-7}$ | $(998, 981, 961)$ | 15.0 |
| 5. Angles | $10^{-9}$ | $5.496 \times 10^{-13}$ | $(998, 998, 958)$ | 1820 |
| 6. Area | $10^{-9}$ | $9.730 \times 10^{-13}$ | $(997, 931, 906)$ | 1028 |

Each figure is the largest residual over every supported triple, which is every $(n_0, n_1, n_2)$ with $1000 \ge n_0 \ge n_1 \ge n_2 \ge 2$ and $1/n_0 + 1/n_1 + 1/n_2 < 1$, 166,665,495 triples. A sweep outside the repository computes them with the code of this crate built in release mode, and no supported triple fails a check. No test in the repository repeats that sweep. The test "Checks at the worst cases" of Section 12.1 runs the six checks on the 294 triples of its set, which include every triple of this table, and prints the same largest residuals, triples, and margins. The margin is the tolerance divided by the largest residual. Every margin is at least 8, as TEST-13 of the coding standard requires.

On a built domain, the third and fourth values of check 3 are 0, because the construction stores the pairing of a mirror as `hyperbolic::reflection(normal)` and the covector as `hyperbolic::covector(normal)`. Its residual is therefore the largest of its first two values, the side test of a vertex against its wall divided by the last coordinate of the vertex, and the convexity term. DOM-30 requires that the convexity term leaves the residual of every reflection domain as the table states, and the test "Checks at the worst cases" prints the largest residuals of this table for the reflection triangles. On a double, the collinear halves of a split side put a vertex on the line of the other half, so the term can add a rounding error there. Section 9.3 states how the accuracy rows of the geometry design relate to the inputs of this crate.

The tolerances are choices of this design, and Section 15.3 asks the owner about that of check 4. The tolerance of check 4 is $2 \times 10^{-6}$, because the largest residual would leave a margin of 7.5 at $10^{-6}$, below the 8 that TEST-13 of the coding standard requires. At $2 \times 10^{-6}$ the margin is 15.0, and check 4 still fails every triangle near the limit with one corner order off by one, by at least 3.09 times its tolerance (Section 8.3). A tolerance of $10^{-5}$ would not separate the order 1000 from 1001 by check 4 alone, because its residual at $(1001, 3, 2)$ labelled `*(1000)32` is $6.277 \times 10^{-6}$.

The tolerance of check 3 stays at $10^{-9}$, the tolerance of checks 1, 2, 5, and 6. A tolerance of $10^{-12}$ would still leave a margin of 192 and would detect a smaller tilt of a wall. This design keeps $10^{-9}$, because one tolerance for these five checks keeps the output of `escher info --check` uniform, and its margin of $1.92 \times 10^{5}$ already exceeds that of every other check.

A Euclidean domain uses the same tolerances. The test "Euclidean domains" of Section 12.1 prints the largest residual of each check over the 72 Euclidean domains of Section 9.4, and every domain passes every check:

| Check | Tolerance | Largest residual | Where | Margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | $10^{-9}$ | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*442` | $2.25 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 2. Pairings | $10^{-9}$ | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*442` | $2.25 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 3. Walls | $10^{-9}$ | $2.220 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*442` | $4.50 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 4. Relators | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $3.146 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*632` | $6.36 \times 10^{9}$ |
| 5. Angles | $10^{-9}$ | $2.220 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*333` | $4.50 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 6. Area | $10^{-9}$ | 0 | Every domain | Not applicable |

A spherical domain uses the same tolerances. The test "Spherical domains" of Section 12.1 prints the largest residual of each check over the 18 spherical domains of Section 9.5, and every domain passes every check:

| Check | Tolerance | Largest residual | Where | Margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | $10^{-9}$ | $1.554 \times 10^{-15}$ | `*522` | $6.43 \times 10^{5}$ |
| 2. Pairings | $10^{-9}$ | $1.554 \times 10^{-15}$ | `*522` | $6.43 \times 10^{5}$ |
| 3. Walls | $10^{-9}$ | $5.551 \times 10^{-16}$ | `*522` | $1.80 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 4. Relators | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $3.097 \times 10^{-14}$ | `*(999)22` | $6.46 \times 10^{7}$ |
| 5. Angles | $10^{-9}$ | $2.038 \times 10^{-14}$ | `*(1000)(1000)` | $4.91 \times 10^{4}$ |
| 6. Area | $10^{-9}$ | $4.075 \times 10^{-14}$ | `*(1000)(1000)` | $2.45 \times 10^{4}$ |

A double uses the same tolerances. The tests "Checks at the worst cases" and "Rotation domains" of Section 12.1 print the largest residual of each check over the doubles of the sets of Section 9.6, and every double passes every check. Every margin is at least 8, as TEST-13 of the coding standard requires:

| Check | Tolerance | Hyperbolic kites: largest residual, where, margin | Euclidean doubles: largest residual, where, margin | Spherical doubles: largest residual, where, margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | $10^{-9}$ | $1.819 \times 10^{-11}$, `(928)43`, 55.0 | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$, `442`, $2.25 \times 10^{6}$ | $1.443 \times 10^{-15}$, `522`, $6.93 \times 10^{5}$ |
| 2. Pairings | $10^{-9}$ | $1.819 \times 10^{-11}$, `(928)43`, 55.0 | $5.551 \times 10^{-16}$, `442`, $1.80 \times 10^{6}$ | $1.443 \times 10^{-15}$, `522`, $6.93 \times 10^{5}$ |
| 3. Walls | $10^{-9}$ | $5.994 \times 10^{-15}$, `(923)65`, $1.67 \times 10^{5}$ | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$, `442`, $2.25 \times 10^{6}$ | $8.882 \times 10^{-16}$, `222`, $1.13 \times 10^{6}$ |
| 4. Relators | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $1.338 \times 10^{-7}$, `(998)(981)(961)`, 15.0. Over the cycles of two vertices only: $1.094 \times 10^{-8}$, `(973)(972)(964)`, 182.8 | $6.661 \times 10^{-16}$, `442`, $3.00 \times 10^{9}$ | $3.097 \times 10^{-14}$, `(999)22`, $6.46 \times 10^{7}$ |
| 5. Angles | $10^{-9}$ | $9.090 \times 10^{-13}$, `(978)(977)(971)`, 1100.1 | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$, `333`, $2.25 \times 10^{6}$ | $7.061 \times 10^{-14}$, `(1000)22`, $1.42 \times 10^{4}$ |
| 6. Area | $10^{-9}$ | $1.946 \times 10^{-12}$, `(997)(931)(906)`, 513.9 | 0 on every double | $4.938 \times 10^{-15}$, `(1000)22`, $2.03 \times 10^{5}$ |

The wall term of check 2 is below the largest residual of check 2 in every column. Over the doubles of Section 9.6, its largest value is $1.046 \times 10^{-12}$ at `(868)44`, a margin of 956, for the kites, $4.996 \times 10^{-16}$ at `442` for the Euclidean doubles, and $8.882 \times 10^{-16}$ at `522` for the spherical doubles. It adds nothing to a reflection domain.

The largest residual of check 4 of the kites is that of a cycle of one vertex, the rotation about a corner of the generating triangle, and equals the largest residual of check 4 of the reflection triangles. The tolerances of this section therefore hold for the doubles unchanged.

A polygon and the double of a polygon use the same tolerances. The test "Checks at the worst cases" of Section 12.1 builds the polygons of the 4430 canonical signatures of the 22 named and the 4421 small polygons of Section 9.7, each counted once, at their default shapes, and their doubles. It runs every check once on each domain, and the guard refuses none of them on the machine of the measurement. It prints the largest residual of each check, with its signature and its margin, for the polygons and for the doubles apart. The column of the doubles leaves out the double of `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, whose smallest margin is below 2. The test prints that double on its own:

| Check | Tolerance | Polygons at the default shape: largest residual, where, margin | Doubles of polygons: largest residual, where, margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1. Isometries | $10^{-9}$ | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$, `*(1000)2222222`, 5.7 | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$, `(1000)2222222`, 5.7 |
| 2. Pairings | $10^{-9}$ | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$, `*(1000)2222222`, 5.7 | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$, `(1000)2222222`, 5.7 |
| 3. Walls | $10^{-9}$ | $4.601 \times 10^{-15}$, `*(1000)2222222`, $2.17 \times 10^{5}$ | $2.647 \times 10^{-14}$, `5555544`, $3.78 \times 10^{4}$ |
| 4. Relators | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $7.673 \times 10^{-9}$, `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, 260.7 | $3.179 \times 10^{-9}$, `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, 629.1 |
| 5. Angles | $10^{-9}$ | $1.956 \times 10^{-13}$, `*(1000)222`, 5112 | $4.116 \times 10^{-13}$, `(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)`, 2430 |
| 6. Area | $10^{-9}$ | $1.779 \times 10^{-13}$, `*(1000)222`, 5622 | $9.521 \times 10^{-13}$, `44444444`, 1050 |

The residual of check 4 of a polygon is that of the double-double check of DOM-49. Rows 1, 2, 3, 5, and 6 of the polygons equal those that a program outside the repository measured with the code of this crate over the same 4430 signatures. Row 4 of the doubles agrees with the sweep of the doubles below.

The test asserts a margin of at least 8 only inside the claims below. It prints the smallest margin inside the claims and overall, and every margin below 8 outside them:

- Polygons: the smallest margin is 5.727, at `*(1000)2222222`, which is outside the claim and is the only polygon below 8. Inside the claim, the smallest margin is 34.36, at `*(1000)2222`.
- Doubles: the smallest margin is 1.003, at `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`. Inside the claim, the smallest margin is 34.36, at `(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)`. Outside the claim, `(1000)2222222`, at 5.727, and `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, at 1.003, are below 8.
- The double at the limit: it builds on the machine of the measurement, with the residuals $5.821 \times 10^{-11}$ of check 1, $1.246 \times 10^{-14}$ of check 3, $1.9936 \times 10^{-6}$ of check 4, a margin of 1.003, and $9.63 \times 10^{-14}$ of check 5.

The smallest margin of each named polygon with a corner of order 1000 at its default is 68.7 for `*(1000)222`, 34.4 for `*(1000)2222`, 5.73 for `*(1000)2222222`, and $1.13 \times 10^{4}$ for `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`. `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)` has no margin below 8, and the margin of its check 4 is 260.7. Every other named polygon has a smallest margin of at least $1.76 \times 10^{4}$, and the smallest margin over the named and the small polygons with orders up to 12 is $1.4 \times 10^{4}$.

This design claims a margin of at least 8 for every check at the default shape of these polygons:

- Every signature with four to eight corners and orders up to 12.
- Every signature with four to six corners and a corner of order 1000.

The test `polygons_of_four_to_six_corners_with_a_corner_of_order_1000_keep_their_claimed_margin` of Section 12.1 asserts the second claim on its own set: every canonical signature with four, five, or six corners, at least one of order 1000, and the other orders from $\{2, 3, 7, 1000\}$, at its default shape. It asserts that each builds, passes every check, and keeps a margin of at least 8, and prints the smallest margin for each number of corners:

| Corners | Signatures | Smallest margin | Where |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 4 | 34 | 68.719 | `*(1000)222` |
| 5 | 97 | 17.180 | `*(1000)2332` |
| 6 | 338 | 11.453 | `*(1000)22322` |

These are the smallest margins that Section 9.7 records for the default sweep with a corner of order 1000, at the same signatures.

The claims rest on the named and the small polygons, the set of this test, and the default sweep of Section 9.7, none of which holds every such signature. The design makes no such claim for a signature with seven or eight corners and a corner of order 1000, where Section 9.7 states the measured smallest margins and the number of signatures of the default sweep below 8. Each of those signatures still passes every check, so `build_with` returns it (DOM-44). The named polygon `*(1000)2222222` is one of them.

A margin below 8 that a later measurement finds inside the claim narrows the claim, and not the set of signatures that DOM-38 admits or the range of DOM-45, because the guard of DOM-44 already refuses every polygon that fails a check. A change MUST NOT raise a tolerance to restore a margin, because the tolerances separate an order from its neighbour at the limit (Section 8.3). This is a choice of this design, which Section 15.3 puts to the owner.

The test "Doubled polygon measurements" of Section 12.1 builds the regular polygons through private access, from vertices rounded once to `f64`, with each wall through two rounded vertices, and doubles them by step 3 of Section 6.8. These walls are not those of the construction of Section 6.10, so the test does not measure the domains that `build_with` returns. Every polygon passes every check. The test requires every double to pass every check, except that a double with every corner of order 1000 may fail check 4, which the test then prints. No measurement records those residuals of check 4 in the arithmetic of DOM-49. Checks 5 and 6 pass on every double (Section 9.7).

A temporary test of this crate, which is not committed, measured the domains that `build_with` returns in a release build, with check 4 as DOM-49 states. It doubled the polygon of each starred signature by Section 6.8 at the default shape, so it also saw the residuals of the doubles that the guard refuses. Its sweep of the doubles holds 75,532 canonical rotation signatures, with the cone orders in descending order:

- Every rotation signature with four to eight orders from 2 to 12 other than `2222`: 1000 with four orders, 3003 with five, 8008 with six, 19,448 with seven, and 43,758 with eight. The rotation signatures of the small polygons of Section 9.7 are a subset of it.
- Every rotation signature with four to eight orders whose first order is 1000 and whose other orders come from $\{1000, 7, 3, 2\}$: 20 with four orders, 35 with five, 56 with six, 84 with seven, and 120 with eight.

The guard refuses no starred polygon of the sweep. Over the starred polygons, the smallest margin of check 4 is $3.4 \times 10^{3}$, $1.7 \times 10^{3}$, 509, 212, and 132 with four to eight orders and an order of 1000, and $2.3 \times 10^{7}$, $9.7 \times 10^{6}$, $3.8 \times 10^{6}$, $1.6 \times 10^{6}$, and $1.0 \times 10^{6}$ with four to eight orders from 2 to 12.

The guard refuses 8 doubles of the sweep, every one by check 4 alone. This table gives the number of refusals and the smallest margin of each check over the doubles that build. Check 2 has the margins of check 1 in every row:

Every named double of Section 9.7 builds on the machine of the measurement. The smallest margin of any check of a named double is 1.003, of check 4, at `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`. The next are 5.73 at `(1000)2222222`, 22.9 at `(1000)2222`, and 34.4 at `(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)`, each of check 1, and every other named double has a smallest margin of at least 45.8.

The refusals come from the storage of the pairings in `f64`, and not from the arithmetic of check 4. A temporary diagnostic evaluated the cycle relators in double-double arithmetic from two sources: the stored pairings, as check 4 does, and the unrounded products of the two stored reflections that each pairing rounds:

| Double | Residual from the stored pairings | Residual from the unrounded products | Largest entry of a pairing |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)` | $1.99 \times 10^{-6}$ | $1.31 \times 10^{-7}$ | 320 |
| `(900)(900)(900)(900)(900)(900)(900)(900)` | $2.92 \times 10^{-6}$ | $2.90 \times 10^{-7}$ | 320 |
| `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)` | $4.07 \times 10^{-8}$ | $5.1 \times 10^{-9}$ | 40.7 |
| `(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)` | $2.5 \times 10^{-10}$ | $1.8 \times 10^{-11}$ | 308 |

A pairing with entries of about 320, rounded to `f64`, keeps an error of about $3 \times 10^{-14}$ in each entry. The cycle of two vertices $(R_j R_1)(R_1 R_{j+1})$ cancels to a small rotation, so this error is large against its result, and the corner frame of a vertex far from the centre and the power of an order up to 1000 amplify it. The pairings are already the compensated products of DOM-25, so no rounding to `f64` of these products removes the error. The renderer folds with the same stored pairings. Evaluated in `f64` instead of by DOM-49, check 4 fails 486 doubles of the first sweep, among them 413 with eight orders from 2 to 12, such as `99999999`.

A rotation signature whose double the guard refuses is therefore refused at its default shape, which is its only shape, with the error `Numerical` and the text of a refused double of Section 10.2. This design raises no tolerance to admit it, because this section forbids raising a tolerance to restore a margin. Removing these refusals needs another form of the stored pairings, which item 40 of Section 15.3 puts to the owner.

This design claims a margin of at least 8 for every check of the doubles of polygons with four to eight orders from 2 to 12, where the sweep holds every such signature and gives a smallest margin of 13.7, of check 1. It claims no margin for the doubles with an order above 12, because no sweep holds every such signature. On the measured sets, the smallest margin of such a double is at least 8 with four or five orders, 8.59 being the smallest, and falls to 5.12 with six orders, 1.27 with seven, and 1.003 with eight, where the guard also refuses some. Each double that `build_with` returns passes every check, which is what the guard asserts (DOM-44). A double whose smallest margin is below 2 can pass or fail with the last bits of its computation, so the tests accept either outcome for it (Section 12.1). Of the named doubles, only `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)` is such a double.

For settings other than the default, the design claims no margin. A setting either builds a polygon that passes every check, or returns `Numerical` (DOM-44). The prototype found no range that keeps a margin of 8 for every setting with seven or eight corners (Section 6.12), and the test "Random polygons" asserts only this alternative.

The ten domains of Section 6.13 use the same tolerances. On a prototype outside the repository, with the real `verify()`, every setting passed every check. The settings were these:

- For each of `o` and `2222`, a grid of `aspect` in $\{0.125, 0.3, 1, 1.618, 4, 8\}$ by `skew` in $\{-0.5, -0.3, 0, 0.25, 0.5\}$ and 2000 random settings, which is 2030 settings.
- For each rectangular domain, the six values of `aspect` and 2000 random values, which is 2006 settings.
- For each kite, its one domain.

On the prototype, the smallest margin is $2.8 \times 10^{5}$, from check 2 on the sheared `2222`. It is the tolerance $10^{-9}$ over the residual $3.6 \times 10^{-15}$. The report of the prototype attributes it to check 4, but its own table gives check 4 a residual of $1.8 \times 10^{-15}$ against $2 \times 10^{-6}$, a margin of about $10^{9}$. The report gives no setting for the residual of check 2, and the last column of the table gives the setting of the largest residual of check 4 only. The margin is far above the 8 of TEST-13 of the coding standard, and no setting of the grid or of the random settings failed a check. The tolerances therefore stay as DOM-9 states. A change MUST NOT loosen a tolerance for these domains. If the crate measures a margin below 8, the change narrows the range of `aspect` or `skew` or changes the construction.

`xx`, `*x`, `22*`, `22x`, and `2*22` have the residual 0 in every check at each of the six values of `aspect` of their grid, so their margin is infinite. The 30 settings of `2222` are the six values of `aspect` by the five values of `skew`. The kites have no parameter, so each has one domain, and its residuals equal those of the prototype. Over the ten domains, the smallest margin is therefore $5.6 \times 10^{5}$, from checks 1 and 2 of `4*2`. The property test of "Wallpaper checks" runs 256 cases, draws the eight domains with a parameter, and asserts only that each domain builds and passes. The grid and the test `kites_build_and_pass` cover the kites, which have one domain each.

Over the kites, the smallest margin is $3.919 \times 10^{3}$ for `2*n`, at `2*(483)`, and $4.007 \times 10^{3}$ for `n*`, at `(883)*`. Both come from check 5 and agree with the prototype. Over the 999 domains of `nx`, which the sweep builds and checks in 0.10 s, the smallest margin is $7.775 \times 10^{3}$, at `(951)x`, from checks 5 and 6. No domain fails a check. At the limits, `(1000)*` has the relator residual $2.7 \times 10^{-14}$ and the angle residual $1.4 \times 10^{-13}$, and `2*(500)` has $5.354 \times 10^{-14}$ and $1.4 \times 10^{-13}$. `(1000)x` has the residuals $2.2 \times 10^{-16}$ in checks 1 and 2, 0 in check 3, $1.6 \times 10^{-17}$ in check 4, and $4.1 \times 10^{-14}$ in checks 5 and 6, a margin of $2.4 \times 10^{4}$, and `5x` has at most $4.4 \times 10^{-16}$ in every check.

The test "Spherical group checks" measured these largest residuals over its set of 46 domains. The set holds the spherical group test set, `(446)*`, `2*(171)`, and `(880)x`, which the fixed seed draws, and `(999)*`, `2*(499)`, and `(999)x`:
