| Relators evaluate | None | Integration test, `presentation` | For the 30 named triples, each read as a reflection and as a rotation signature, for `*632`, `*442`, `*333`, `*2222`, `632`, `442`, `333`, and `2222`, for the ten domains of Section 6.13 at `aspect` 1, 0.125, and 8, and for `o` and `2222` also at `skew` $-0.5$, 0.25, and 0.5, for the named polygons of Section 9.7 and their doubles other than `(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, whose smallest margin is below 2 (Section 8.4), for the spherical signatures of "Exhaustive certificate" and "Exhaustive certificate at the limit", and for the spherical group domains of "Spherical certificate" and "Spherical certificate at the limit": every relator of the side presentation evaluates to the identity under the convention of DOM-32. Each letter $s_i$ is the matrix $P_i^{-1}$, by `hyperbolic::inverse` or `spherical::inverse`, and for a Euclidean domain by the matrix inverse of `glam`. The base is multiplied left to right. A corner relator or a cycle relator is evaluated as check 4 evaluates it: for a hyperbolic domain in the corner frame of the vertex of its corner or of the start vertex of its cycle, with the power by successive multiplication and the residual divided by the exponent, against the tolerance of check 4. For a hyperbolic domain with five or more vertices, whose check 4 runs in double-double arithmetic (DOM-49), the test evaluates the corner and cycle relators exactly instead, in the reference arithmetic of Section 12.2 with an exact frame. A square and a pair relator are evaluated with no frame, as the residual of check 2, against the tolerance of check 2. The test classifies each relator by the kinds of the sides of its domain and the rules of DOM-33, DOM-34, and DOM-52, and not by `reverses_orientation()`, which is true for wallpaper domains that are not all mirrors. A relator that the presentation leaves out is never evaluated, so this test does not catch a dropped square of a mirror. The tests of the exact relator texts, "Side presentation table", "Wallpaper presentations", and "Spherical presentations", catch it, and the counts of "Equal counts" and "Spherical counts" do not. It prints the largest residual of each kind of relator, with its signature, its margin, and the number of relators of the kind, over all its domains and over the named reflection polygons apart. The table below DOM-36 records the largest residuals over all its domains, none of which is at a wallpaper domain, and the paragraph after that table gives the largest residual of the wallpaper domains (DOM-19) |

The base of the cycle relator `s2 s4 s0`, whose cycle visits three sides, evaluates under the convention of DOM-32 within the tolerance of check 4, while the reversed base, and the base under the opposite convention in which $s_i$ is $P_i$, each give a residual of more than 8 times that tolerance. | Wallpaper cycles | The ten domains, of Section 5.10 of the plan | Unit test `walk_finds_the_cycles_of_the_built_wallpaper_domains` in `verify.rs`, on the ten domains that `build_with` returns | The walk is private, so no integration test reads the cycles, and "Wallpaper presentations" covers them through the relator texts. The walk of DOM-55 finds the cycles of the table of DOM-50, with the start state, the order, the states, and the sides of every cycle as Section 14 lists them for `aspect` 1, and the domain has the closure of Section 3.2. The walk by `walk_from` from the flipped start state of each cycle gives the flipped start state followed by the flipped states of the cycle in reverse order. A cycle and its flipped orbit are either the same set, as for a chain, or disjoint. Counting the states of each cycle, and of its flipped orbit when that differs from the cycle, counts each of the $2m$ states exactly once. The orders above 1 of the cycles that visit no mirror equal `cone_orders()` of the signature, and the orders above 1 of the chains equal its corner orders, each as a multiset. The sum of $1/m_c$ over the cycles that visit no mirror and of $1/(2 m_c)$ over the chains, minus the number of pairs, minus half the number of mirrors, plus 1, is 0 within $10^{-15}$, the Euler characteristic of a Euclidean signature. This last identity is this design's reading of the Euler characteristic of the glued domain, which no source states in this form (Section 14). The prototype found it 0 for all ten domains. The test asserts the states at `aspect` 1, for `o` at `skew` 0 and 0.25 and for `2222` at `skew` 0, 0.25, and $-0.5$, and the coverage of the states and the identity at `aspect` 1, 0.125, and 8, for `o` and `2222` also at `skew` $-0.5$, 0.25, and 0.5. A kite has no parameter, so the test checks its states, their coverage, and the identity once, outside the loop over these values |

The linear parts of the elements form the point group, of the order 1, 2, 2, 2, 4, 4, 4, 8, 6, and 2 for `o`, `**`, `xx`, `*x`, `22x`, `22*`, `2*22`, `4*2`, `3*3`, and `2222`. The translations form a lattice whose covolume equals that order times the area of the domain within $10^{-12}$. The orders above 1 of the rotations whose centres lie on no mirror line are the cone orders of the signature, and those of the rotations whose centres lie on a mirror line are its corner orders, each as a multiset. The test counts the classes of rotation centres up to the group: it takes the centres within $R/2$ of the centre of the domain, merges two centres when an element maps one onto the other, and keeps the largest order at a centre. The group has a reflection exactly when the signature has a boundary, and a glide reflection whose axis is no mirror line exactly for `xx`, `*x`, `22x`, `22*`, `2*22`, `4*2`, and `3*3` (DOM-57). The test asserts the point group of the order 1 for `o`, 2 for `**`, `xx`, `*x`, and `2222`, 4 for `22x`, `22*`, and `2*22`, 8 for `4*2`, and 6 for `3*3`. It asserts a reflection for `**`, `*x`, `22*`, `2*22`, `4*2`, and `3*3` only, and a glide reflection whose axis is no mirror line for `xx`, `*x`, `22x`, `22*`, `2*22`, `4*2`, and `3*3` only. It asserts the cone orders $[2]$ for `22x` and `2*22`, $[2, 2]$ for `22*`, $[2, 2, 2, 2]$ for `2222`, which are the four classes of its half-turn centres, $[4]$ for `4*2`, $[3]$ for `3*3`, and none for the others. It asserts the corner orders $[2, 2]$ for `2*22`, $[2]$ for `4*2`, $[3]$ for `3*3`, and none for the others. So the point group tells `o` from the others, `22x` from `**`, `xx`, and `*x`, and `22*` from `2222`. The glide reflection tells `**` from `xx` and `*x`, and the reflection tells `*x` from `xx` and `22*` from `22x`. The corner orders tell `22*` from `2*22`. Measured on the crate: 113 elements within $R$ at every setting of `**`, `xx`, `*x`, `22*`, `22x`, and `2*22`, and of `o` except at `skew` $-0.5$ and 0.5, which keep 111. `2222` keeps 61 at `aspect` 1 and `skew` 0, 55 at `aspect` 0.125 and 8 and at `skew` 0.25, and 57 at `skew` $-0.5$ and 0.5. `4*2` keeps 111 and `3*3` keeps 190. The covolume error is 0 except for `o` at `aspect` 8, $8.9 \times 10^{-16}$, `*x` at `aspect` 8, $8.88 \times 10^{-16}$, `2222` at `aspect` 0.125 and 8, $8.88 \times 10^{-16}$, `4*2`, $9.77 \times 10^{-15}$, and `3*3`, $2.49 \times 10^{-14}$ |
| Kite certificate | The ten domains, of Section 5.10 of the plan | Integration test `wallpaper_certificate_tells_the_kites_from_the_groups_of_their_triangles`, `wallpaper` | Each kite and each of the six controls `*442`, `442`, `*333`, `333`, `632`, and `*632` goes through the helper `certified` of "Wallpaper certificate". The features are the order of the point group, the reflection, the rotation centres off the mirror lines, and those on the mirror lines. Against `4*2`, `*442` differs in both kinds of centre only, with the same point group 8 and a reflection, and `442` differs in all four. Against `3*3`, `*333` differs in both kinds of centre only, with the same point group 6 and a reflection, `333` differs in all four, `632` differs in the reflection and both kinds of centre, with the same point group 6, and `*632` differs in the point group and both kinds of centre, with a reflection. Measured on the crate: `*442` has a reflection and the corner orders $[2, 4, 4]$, `442` the cone orders $[2, 4, 4]$, `*333` the corner orders $[3, 3, 3]$, `333` the cone orders $[3, 3, 3]$, `632` the cone orders $[2, 3, 6]$, and `*632` the corner orders $[2, 3, 6]$ |

The pairing of side 0 equals that of side 0 of the built `*442` or `*632`, the pairing of side 1 equals `euclidean::reflection` of its covector, and the pairings of sides 2 and 3 equal those of the built `442` or `632`, each bit for bit. "Wallpaper defects", "Glide check 2", the part of "Invalid pairing" with mirrors beside paired sides, "Mirror relators", and the chain part of "Long cycle word" run on the domains that `build_with` returns. Where a test sets a defect, it sets it through private access.

The wallpaper domains require changes to these existing tests, because they assert behaviour that DOM-28, DOM-35, and DOM-52 change:

- The unit tests `presentation::tests::causes_are_checked_in_order`, `presentation::tests::reflection_triangle_with_a_paired_side_gives_invalid_pairing`, and `verify::tests::verify_fails_on_a_double_side_labelled_as_a_mirror`. The first expects the text of the number of corners. The second expects the text of a walk that does not close. In the third, check 4 passes, and checks 2 and 3 fail. On the prototype, these were the only 3 of 903 tests of the workspace that failed, and every golden image, snapshot, and relator text passed (Section 14).
- The unit tests of the error text in `error.rs`: `display_of_each_kind_starts_with_the_operation_and_is_lower_case`, `display_of_each_kind_is_the_documented_text`, `kind_matches_each_constructor`, and `source_is_the_group_error_only_for_a_rejected_presentation`. Their list of `InvalidPairing` errors has no cause of mixed sides, because DOM-35 makes a domain with mirror and paired sides valid.
- The test helpers that build a `FundamentalDomain` through private access: `labelled`, `spherical`, `labelled_kite`, and `polygon_from_vertices` in `verify.rs`, and `hexagon` in `presentation.rs`. Each sets `closure: Closure::PerVertex` (Section 3.2), and no assertion changes. The helpers `labelled_kite` and `doubled` in `verify.rs` pass `Closure::PerVertex` to `double()`.
- The unit test `walk_finds_the_vertex_cycles_of_each_double` in `verify.rs`, which reads the vertices of each cycle from its states. Its expectations do not change.
- The unit tests `double_whose_cycle_copies_do_not_sum_to_two_gives_invalid_pairing` and `verify_fails_on_a_double_with_wrong_copies` do not change, because DOM-55 keeps the condition on the copies for the doubles.
- "Unsupported" in `tests/domain/unsupported.rs`, which lists each wallpaper signature as one that builds when `build_with` builds its domain, as its row states.
- The helper of "Relators evaluate" in `tests/domain/presentation.rs`, which classifies the relators by the kinds of the sides and not by `reverses_orientation()`.
- "Any signature" in `tests/domain/properties.rs`, whose generator writes handles and cross-caps, reaches the wallpaper domains, and its assertion that a returned domain passes `verify()` covers them with no change.

The spherical group domains require changes to these existing tests of this crate, each of which asserts that a signature that Section 4 supports has no domain. On the prototype of Section 14, with every new signature built and no other change, these were the tests of this crate that failed, and no other test of this crate failed:

- `unsupported::build_rejects_every_unsupported_signature` in `tests/domain/unsupported.rs`, which lists `3*2`, `2*`, `x`, `1`, and `*` as unsupported. They move to the list of signatures that build, as the row "Unsupported" states.
- `parameters::build_with_rejects_an_unsupported_signature_before_its_settings` in `tests/domain/parameters.rs`, which uses `3*2` and takes `4*3` in its place.
- `triangle::tests::supported_rejects_a_signature_without_a_construction`, which uses `3*2` and `*` and takes `4*3` in their place, as the row "Corner order and area" states.
- `domain::tests::build_with_settings_checks_the_signature_and_the_limit_first`, which uses `3*2` and takes `4*3` in its place.
- The doctest of `Error` in `src/error.rs`, which uses `3*2` as the unsupported signature and takes `4*3` in its place, as Section 10.2 does.

The unit tests of `src/error.rs` that build `Error::unsupported` from `3*2` directly do not fail, because they build no domain, and they may keep `3*2`. The tests of `escher-cli` and `escher-render` that use `3*2`, `*`, `x`, `1`, `2*`, or `2*(12)` as a signature without a domain belong to `docs/design/cli.md` and `docs/design/render.md`. The test helpers of this crate that build a `FundamentalDomain` through private access with `Closure::Wallpaper` take `Closure::Cycles`.

The spherical kites also change these tests, and every assertion that they had holds:

- The helper `assert_each_state_is_covered_once` of `verify.rs` calls `assert_states_cover_the_characteristic` with the Euler characteristic 0. "Spherical cycles" calls the second helper with the positive characteristic of each kite.
- The unit test `only_the_double_of_2222_sums_the_angles_of_its_cycles` of `verify.rs` also asserts the closure `Cycles` of `3*2`, `2*3`, `3*`, and `2*`.
- The unit tests of the error text in `error.rs` gain the rows of the text `order 501 is larger than 500` and of the text of the spherical guard.
- "Relators evaluate" in `tests/domain/presentation.rs` also evaluates the relators of the kites of the spherical group test set. No kite sets one of its largest residuals.
- `tests/domain/spherical.rs` makes the helpers of "Exhaustive certificate" and their tolerances `pub(crate)`, so that `spherical_groups` reuses them.

"Any signature" reaches the spherical group domains through its generator, and its assertion that a returned domain passes `verify()` covers them with no change.

These are the times of the spherical group tests in a debug build. "In `just test`" is one run of `just test` with the load of the whole run, and "Alone" is the test run on its own. The column "Kites" gives the times before the lune groups were added, and the column "With the lune groups" the times of the tests that cover them, from a later run of `just test`:

| Test | Kites, in `just test` | Kites, alone | With the lune groups, in `just test` |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Spherical sweep | 0.345 s | 0.14 s for `n*` and 0.04 s for `2*n` | 1.415 s, of which 0.10 s for `nx` |
| Spherical certificate, lune groups | | | 0.237 s |
| Spherical certificate at the limit | 3.098 s | 1.52 s | 3.062 s for the lune groups, and 0.56 s alone for `(1000)x` |
| Spherical side orders | 0.900 s | 0.62 s | 3.501 s |
| Spherical group accuracy, lune groups | | | 0.042 s |

In "Spherical side orders", the slowest enumeration of the kites is that of `2*(500)`, at 140 to 148 ms, and the enumerations of the kites take 0.24 to 0.25 s. With the lune groups, the slowest enumeration is that of `(1000)x`, at 167.653 ms, and all the enumerations take 0.409 s. Every presentation without one of its cycle relators, `5x` without `(s1 s1)^5` and `x` without `s1 s1` included, stops at the coset limit. In "Spherical counts", each search of the side or the Conway presentation takes at most 0.008 s. The searches without a cycle relator run to 6 colours and not to 16, because a search to 16 colours took up to 23 s, for `3*2` without `s2 s0 s3 s1`. Up to 6 colours, the counts of `3*2` without that relator are 1, 3, 10, 57, 60, and 504, against 1, 1, 1, 4, 0, and 7 of the Conway presentation. Those of `5x` without `(s1 s1)^5` are 1, 1, 1, 1, 1, and 1, against 1, 1, 0, 0, 1, and 0. Each search of a lune group takes at most 0.001 s. The comparison of the side and the Conway presentations runs to 16 colours or the group order.

DOM-57. The tests "Wallpaper certificate" and "Equal counts" MUST cover each of the ten domains of Section 6.13 and MUST stay while `UNV-11` of the plan is open, and the certificate MUST assert the point group and the glide reflections whose axes are not mirror lines, because no source states that the pairings of these domains generate the group of the signature, and the counts alone cannot show it. On the prototype, the counts of `o` and `xx` were equal at every index that it computed. For `xx`, it computed every index up to 64. For `o`, the default budget stopped the search at index 39 on the side presentation and at index 32 on the Conway presentation. Dropping $s_1^2$ or $s_3^2$ from the side presentation of `*x` left its counts at 1, 3, 4, 11, 6, and 12, while dropping a cycle relator gave 1, 3, 7, 23, 71, and 255. So the counts catch a dropped cycle relator. The test "Wallpaper presentations", which asserts the exact relator texts, catches a dropped square of a mirror. Only the certificate tells `o` from `xx`. Neither test closes `UNV-11`, which a source or a Lean proof closes.

DOM-64. The tests "Spherical certificate", "Spherical certificate at the limit", "Census defects", "Spherical side orders", and "Spherical counts" MUST cover every spherical group domain of their sets and MUST stay while `UNV-34` of the plan is open. The certificate MUST assert the order by the breadth-first closure, the exactly-once tiling of the sample, and the census of each group, and the census MUST NOT be left out, because no source states that the pairings of these domains generate the group of the signature, and the order and the presentation do not separate these groups. `432` and `*332` are isomorphic as abstract groups, and so are `2*n` and `(2n)22`, and `*nn` and `n22`. `n*`, `nx`, `n22`, and `*nn` all have the order $2n$, and `x` and `*` have equal tiles and equal colourings. Neither `verify()` nor the enumeration of the side presentation tells `n*` from `2*(n/2)`, `nx` from `*nn` and from `(2n)(2n)`, or `2*n` from `(2n)*`, so the census and the exactly-once test of the certificate are the tests against these mix-ups.

The census of a group counts, over the elements of its closure, the rotations, which include the identity, the elements that reverse orientation, the reflections, the presence of $-I$, and the axes of the rotations other than the identity, as the order of the rotation times the number of axes with that order, with the number of those axes that lie on a mirror plane in brackets. A rotation is an element with the determinant 1, a reflection an element with the determinant $-1$ that is an involution with a fixed plane, and an axis is the line of the fixed points of a rotation. These are the censuses that the prototype of Section 14 found, which the test asserts. The prototype confirmed row by row the closed forms below for the columns of the rotations, the reversing elements, the reflections, and $-I$ of the families. No research document states these values, so they rest on `UNV-34` of the plan:

| Signature | Order | Rotations | Reversing | Reflections | $-I$ | Axes |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `1` | 1 | 1 | 0 | 0 | No | None |
| `*` | 2 | 1 | 1 | 1 | No | None |
| `x` | 2 | 1 | 1 | 0 | Yes | None |
| `2*` | 4 | 2 | 2 | 1 | Yes | 2x1 (0) |
| `2x` | 4 | 2 | 2 | 0 | No | 2x1 (0) |
| `3*` | 6 | 3 | 3 | 1 | No | 3x1 (0) |
| `3x` | 6 | 3 | 3 | 0 | Yes | 3x1 (0) |
| `4*` | 8 | 4 | 4 | 1 | Yes | 4x1 (0) |
| `4x` | 8 | 4 | 4 | 0 | No | 4x1 (0) |
| `2*2` | 8 | 4 | 4 | 2 | No | 2x3 (1) |
| `2*3` | 12 | 6 | 6 | 3 | Yes | 3x1 (1), 2x3 (0) |
| `3*2` | 24 | 12 | 12 | 3 | Yes | 3x4 (0), 2x3 (3) |
| `(1000)*` | 2000 | 1000 | 1000 | 1 | Yes | 1000x1 (0) |
| `(1000)x` | 2000 | 1000 | 1000 | 0 | No | 1000x1 (0) |
| `2*(500)` | 2000 | 1000 | 1000 | 500 | No | 500x1 (1), 2x500 (0) |

For the families, the test asserts these closed forms, which the prototype confirmed for $n$ up to 12 and at the limits:

| Family | Rotations | Reversing | Reflections | $-I$ |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `n*` | $n$ | $n$ | 1 | Exactly when $n$ is even |
| `nx` | $n$ | $n$ | 0 | Exactly when $n$ is odd |
| `2*n` | $2n$ | $2n$ | $n$ | Exactly when $n$ is odd |

The axes of the families follow the rows of the first table: `n*` and `nx` have one axis of order $n$, off every mirror plane, and `2*n` has one axis of order $n$ on its mirror planes and $n$ axes of order 2 off them. On the prototype, neither the full census nor its four counts without the axes gave the same values to two of the 90 spherical groups that it compared, and the closure had exactly `group_order()` elements for all 37 canonical spherical group signatures with $n$ up to 12 and for the three limits. The smallest distance between two elements of a closure was $4.2 \times 10^{-3}$, at `(1000)*`, far above the merge tolerance of the test. The crate measures a different quantity, the smallest distance between two images of the centre, which the rows "Spherical certificate" and "Spherical certificate at the limit" give. Over 4000 points of a Fibonacci lattice and up to 253 near-vertex and near-side points for each domain, every point had exactly one element that mapped it inside the domain, and every point folded to that image.

These five domains of wrong groups pass `verify()` and enumerate to the order of their label on the prototype, and the two of `nx` do so on the crate too. Only the census, or the exactly-once test, tells them from the group of their label. They are the negative tests of the census in "Census defects":

| Domain of a wrong group | Labelled as | Group that it has | `verify()` | Enumeration | Census |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| A lune of the angle of `nx` paired by the rotations through $\pm\pi/n$, with the stored orders $[2n, 2n]$, which the test takes from the public build of `(2n)(2n)` | `nx` | `(2n)(2n)` | Passes | $2n$, equal | Differs |
| The lune of `nx` with mirror sides, with the area of `nx`, which the test takes from the public build of `*nn` | `nx` | `*nn` | Passes | $2n$, equal | Differs |
| The kite of `422` with mirrors on sides 0 and 1 and the corners $[n/2, 1, 2, 1]$ | `4*` | `2*2` | Passes | 8, equal | Differs |
| The kite of `622` with mirrors on sides 0 and 1 and the corners $[n/2, 1, 2, 1]$ | `6*` | `2*3` | Passes | 12, equal | Differs |
| The kite of `622` with mirrors on sides 2 and 3 | `2*3` | `6*` | Passes | 12, equal | Differs |

The label `GlideReflection` on the pairings of `x` also passed `verify()` and enumerated to 2 on the prototype without the plane rule, with the same census, because it changes a label and not a group. The plane rule of DOM-61 catches it (Section 8.3).

On the prototype, dropping one mirror relator from the side presentation left the enumeration at `group_order()` and the colouring counts equal to those of the Conway presentation, on each signature that it tried: $s_0^2$ and $s_1^2$ of `3*2` and `2*3`, $s_2^2$ and $s_3^2$ of `3*` and `2*`, and any one of the four relators of `*`. The tests of the group therefore drop cycle relators only. On the prototype, dropping a cycle relator stopped the enumeration at the coset limit and changed the counts, for `3*2`, `3*`, `2*3`, `5x`, and `2*`. For `x`, dropping `s1 s1` was caught by the enumeration alone, because the counts to 2 colours were $[1, 1]$ with and without it. A relator that the presentation leaves out is never evaluated, so "Relators evaluate" cannot catch it. The test "Spherical presentations" asserts the full list of relators of each domain, mirror relators included, so it catches a relator that is left out. None of these tests closes `UNV-34`, which a source or a Lean proof closes.

DOM-22. The tests "Exhaustive certificate" and "Exhaustive certificate at the limit" MUST cover every lune of their sets and MUST stay while `UNV-29` is open, because no source makes the lune a fundamental domain (Section 6.7), and these tests are the only evidence that its two reflections generate a group of order $2n$ whose copies of the lune cover the sphere.

DOM-31. The tests "Exhaustive certificate" and "Exhaustive certificate at the limit" MUST cover every spherical double of their sets, `332`, `432`, `532`, `n22`, and `nn`, and MUST stay while `UNV-22` or `UNV-30` is open, because no statement in orbifold notation gives the signature of the group that the pairings of a double generate, no source makes the double of a lune a fundamental domain, and the enumeration of the group with `group_order()` elements and the dense sample are the only evidence for both on the sphere.

DOM-48. The tests of the polygons MUST include the tests "Cut meaning", "Walk against weld", and "Closing side", and the identities of "Polygons" and "Default shape" that share no formula with Section 6.10: the angles $\pi/n_j$ and the area $-2\pi\chi$ measured from the reference vertices, and the tangency of the inscribed polygon. The reason is that the reference of Section 12.2 follows the steps of Section 6.10 in another arithmetic, so it shares their formulas, which rest on `UNV-6`, `UNV-7`, and `UNV-27`, and a wrong formula would pass a comparison with the reference alone.

DOM-36. The test "Relators evaluate" MUST cover every domain of its sets, hyperbolic, Euclidean, and spherical, reflection, rotation, and wallpaper, and the test "Long cycle word" MUST exist, and both MUST stay while `UNV-32` is open, because no source states the cycle relators as words in the convention of DOM-32, and a reversed relator or the opposite convention presents a group of the same order, which no enumeration detects.

The two tests establish different things. "Relators evaluate" establishes that every relator of every domain of its sets evaluates to the identity under the convention of DOM-32, within the tolerances of checks 2 and 4. It does not fix the convention, because every vertex cycle of its reflection domains and doubles visits one or two sides (Section 5.4), and on the prototype of Section 14 the reversed words and the opposite convention gave the same residuals. "Long cycle word" establishes, for the one cycle of three sides of one hexagon, that the base in walk order under the convention of DOM-32 evaluates to the identity and that its reversal and the opposite convention do not. It does not establish that the walk order and the convention give the cycle relator of every domain, such as the wallpaper domains, whose cycles apply up to four sides and pass mirrors. The test also runs on the four-letter cycle 1 of `22*`, `s2 s1 s5 s0`, on the domain that `build_with` returns. There it asserts the reading of DOM-32 and only prints the other two readings, for the reason that the next paragraph gives (Section 5.4).

A measurement on `**`, `xx`, `*x`, and `22*` at `aspect` 1, 0.125, and 8, with `**`, `xx`, and `*x` built by hand with the same pairings that `build_with` gives them, found that on every cycle of three or more letters the base in walk order under the convention of DOM-32, the reversed base, and the base under the opposite convention each give the residual exactly 0. No committed test repeats that measurement, except on cycle 1 of the built `22*` at `aspect` 1, where the test prints `walk 0e0, reversed 0e0, opposite convention 0e0, tolerance 2e-6`. These cycles do not tell the readings apart, so the hexagon is the one domain on which the other two readings fail. Neither test closes `UNV-32`, which a source or a Lean proof closes.

These are the largest residuals of "Relators evaluate", measured on the crate over the triangles, the Euclidean and spherical domains, their doubles, the named polygons of Section 9.7, their doubles other than the one at the limit, and the ten wallpaper domains. The count of relators of each kind covers all of these domains:

| Kind of relator | Largest residual | Where | Tolerance | Margin | Relators |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Square | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$ | `*(1000)2222222` | $10^{-9}$ | 5.7 | 333 |
| Corner | $1.338 \times 10^{-7}$ | `*(998)(981)(961)` | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | 15.0 | 302 |
| Pair | $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$ | `(1000)2222222` | $10^{-9}$ | 5.7 | 271 |
| Cycle | $1.338 \times 10^{-7}$ | `(998)(981)(961)` | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | 15.0 | 375 |

No wallpaper domain sets a largest residual of this table. The test also prints these largest residuals for the wallpaper domains alone, over their settings of the test:

| Kind of relator | Largest residual | Where | Tolerance | Margin | Relators |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Square | $1.776 \times 10^{-15}$ | `4*2` | $10^{-9}$ | $5.6 \times 10^{5}$ | 31 |
| Pair | $4.441 \times 10^{-16}$ | `4*2` | $10^{-9}$ | $2.3 \times 10^{6}$ | 59 |
| Cycle | $1.332 \times 10^{-15}$ | `4*2` | $2 \times 10^{-6}$ | $1.5 \times 10^{9}$ | 81 |

The test prints no row of corner relators for the wallpaper domains, because they give none. On the prototype of Section 14, each cycle word of the ten domains, raised to its order, evaluated to the identity within $1.8 \times 10^{-15}$.

The corner residual at `*(998)(981)(961)` equals the residual in the corner frame of the table of Section 8.2. The largest cycle residual is that of a kite, so no cycle relator of a double of a polygon in the set exceeds it. Without the doubles of polygons, the largest pair residual is $1.819 \times 10^{-11}$ at `(928)43`, a margin of 55.0. Over the 22 named reflection polygons alone, the largest square residual is $1.746 \times 10^{-10}$ at `*(1000)2222222`, a margin of 5.7, over 130 relators, and the largest corner residual is $7.673 \times 10^{-9}$ at `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)`, a margin of 260.7, over 130 relators, from the exact evaluation in the reference. The square residual of `*(1000)2222222` and the pair residual of `(1000)2222222` have margins below 8, which are the smallest margins of check 1 at those signatures (Section 8.4). The test asserts the tolerances of checks 2 and 4 and sets no bound of its own, so these margins change no assertion.

The hexagon of "Long cycle word" is cut from the kite of `732`, whose vertices are $W_0$ to $W_3$, with $P_0$ the pairing of side 0, about $W_0$, and $Q$ the pairing of side 3, about $W_2$. The point $X$ lies on side 3 of the kite, at 0.37 of the way from $W_2$ to $W_3$, normalized. The triangle $(W_3, W_0, X)$ is cut off along $W_0 X$ and pasted across side 1 by $P_0$. The hexagon has these values:

- Vertices: $W_0$, $P_0 X$, $W_1$, $Q X$, $W_2$, and $X$.
- Pairings, by side: $P_0$, $P_0^{-1}$, $Q P_0^{-1}$, $P_0 Q^{-1}$, $Q^{-1}$, and $Q$, with the pairs (0, 1), (2, 3), and (4, 5), each of the kind `Rotation`.
- Corners, as order and copies: (7, 2), (1, 1), (2, 2), (1, 1), (3, 2), and (1, 0).
- Vertex cycles: vertices 0, 2, and 4 are cycles of one vertex, and vertices 1, 3, and 5 form one cycle of order 1, whose walk applies sides 2, 4, and 0.

Measured residuals of the cycle relator `s2 s4 s0`, against the tolerance $2 \times 10^{-6}$ of check 4:

| Reading | Residual |
| --- | --- |
| Base in walk order, under the convention of DOM-32 | $2.44 \times 10^{-15}$ |
| Base reversed, under the convention of DOM-32 | 1.037 |
| Base in walk order, under the opposite convention, with $s_i$ read as $P_i$ | 1.060 |

Vertex 5 of the hexagon has 0 copies, because check 4 requires the copies of a cycle to sum to 2 (DOM-55), and a cycle of three vertices cannot meet that with copies of at least 1. The copies 1, 1, and 0 of vertices 1, 3, and 5 sum to 2 but do not describe their angles, so the test asserts check 4 only. Item 33 of Section 15.3 puts the point to the owner.

A regular hyperbolic quadrilateral with its opposite sides paired, whose one vertex cycle visits four sides, was measured as a candidate domain for this test and does not serve. The residual of its reversed base was $8.7 \times 10^{-10}$ and that of its base under the opposite convention $3.2 \times 10^{-8}$, both within the tolerance of check 4, so neither reading failed. No test uses the quadrilateral.

"Relators evaluate" evaluates a hyperbolic corner or cycle relator in the corner frame of check 4, because without the frame the residual at the largest orders fails the tolerance of check 4 by far, as the prototype figures of Section 14 show.

The fold of the two certificate tests is a private copy in the tests of this crate. For every finite point other than the zero vector it applies the same rule as the fold of `escher-render` (Section 5.3 of `docs/design/render.md`). It differs only on degenerate input: it has no stop for a point that is not finite or for the zero vector, and it treats a NaN side value as inside the side, where the fold of `escher-render` returns an unconverged result for each of these. Side $i$ is violated at a point $x$ when $s_i > \tau \, \sigma_i \, \lambda(x)$, where $s_i$ is `spherical::side` of the covector of the side at $x$, $\tau = 4 \times 10^{-15}$ is a copy of the constant of `escher-render`, $\sigma_i = \sum_j \lvert c_{ij} \rvert$ for the covector $c_i$, and $\lambda(x) = \max_j \lvert x_j \rvert$. The fold applies the pairing of the first violated side, and stops when no side is violated. A fold that applies the pairing of the first side with a positive side value does not stop for a point on a wall. On `*33`, the point $(0.14514, 0.95694, 0.25139)$ of side 0 has the side value $+2.78 \times 10^{-17}$, and the reflection maps it to itself bit for bit, so such a fold cycles until the cap. The images of the centre lie far from every wall, so the tolerance does not change their depth.

The floors are set from the smallest distance measured between two images of the centre, with a margin of at least 8 as TEST-13 of the coding standard requires:

| Test | Floor | Smallest distance | Where | Margin |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Exhaustive certificate | $2 \times 10^{-2}$ | 0.2168 | `*532` | 10.8 |
| Exhaustive certificate at the limit | $2 \times 10^{-4}$ | $2.810 \times 10^{-3}$ | `*(1000)22` | 14.0 |
| Exhaustive certificate, doubles | $4 \times 10^{-2}$ | 0.4472 | `532` | 11.2 |
| Exhaustive certificate at the limit, doubles | $4 \times 10^{-4}$ | $5.620 \times 10^{-3}$ | `(1000)22` | 14.0 |

At the limit, `*(1000)22` has the group order 4000 and `*(1000)(1000)` the order 2000. The largest breadth-first depth there is about 1001, at `*(1000)22`, below the default largest number of folds of 1500.

The claim that a fold returns in its breadth-first depth and never reaches the cap holds for the images of the centre of a reflection domain, which the tests assert. For a double, the fold can take more steps than the breadth-first depth, so the tests assert only that it takes at least as many steps and converges within the cap. Each test prints the largest number of steps above the depth over its doubles: at most 3, at `532`, for orders up to 12, and 0 at the limit. It does not hold for every point: near a vertex of very high order, a fold can fail to converge, which belongs to the unverified statements on termination, `UNV-1` and, for a double, `UNV-2` (Section 14).

The plan item "Properties" also names the inverse $J M^t J$ and the round trip of the disk map. Those are properties of `escher-geometry`, and the geometry design tests them.

The expected values for $\{7, 3\}$ come from the split of the polygon into $2p$ right triangles (Section 10.1 of the geometry reference), which puts the centre of the polygon at vertex 0 of `*732`, a vertex of the polygon at vertex 1, and the midpoint of an edge at vertex 2. The tests evaluate equations 29 and 24 of [HT] (Section 10.4 of the geometry reference) in the reference arithmetic, with the curvature radius $\ell = 1$. Computed from those equations, the edge length is 0.566256306735315 and the circumradius in the disk is 0.300742618746379. The tests measure an error of $1.548 \times 10^{-16}$ in the edge against its bound of $2 \times 10^{-13}$, and of $2.025 \times 10^{-16}$ in the circumradius against its bound of $10^{-13}$. No value that Table 2 of [HT] prints serves as an expected value, because that table and equation 29 disagree on the edge length of $\{7, 3\}$ (item 23 of Section 15.1 of the geometry reference).

DOM-14. A test MUST NOT take an expected value from Table 2 of [HT], because the research records that the table and equation 29 disagree for $\{7, 3\}$, and a test built on the wrong one passes a wrong domain or fails a correct one.

The property tests commit their regression files (TEST-9 of the coding standard). Every property test, "Random polygons" included, takes its configuration from the one helper `proptest_config()` in `common`, which returns the default configuration of `proptest`.

The tests compare a value that passes through a transcendental function in three ways:

- With a stored value, bit for bit:
  - "Angle sum of a pentagon" compares its angles exactly with 0 and $\pi$. Every corner frame of its pentagon is exact, and each angle is the `atan2` of an exact 0 and a non-zero coordinate. The test assumes that `atan2` then gives 0 or the `f64` value of $\pi$ in the platform's library. The test checks this assumption, and no source cited here establishes it.
  - "Polygon parameters" compares the defaults of the polygons, which the crate rounds to three decimals, exactly with the literal defaults of Section 6.6. The exact comparison holds only while each unrounded default lies further from a boundary of the rounding than its error, which Section 9.7 measures.
- With another result of the same run, bit for bit: "Generating domain", "Skew zero", and the tests of the small and of the named doubles of "Rotation polygons" compare two builds (DOM-30). "Cycle rule of `2222`" compares the residual of check 5 by the cycle rule with the residual by the per-vertex rule, and with the residual that `verify()` reports. Both rules take the corner angles from the same function on the same domain in one run, so each arccosine has the same argument and the same result.
- Within a tolerance, as TEST-13 of the coding standard requires, in every other test. For example, "Euclidean table", "Parameters", "Spherical table", and "Rotation table" compare two builds of one domain within twice the vertex bound of Section 9.4 or 9.5, or twice the image-vertex bound of Section 9.6. "Rotation table" finds a flat vertex within the corner-angle bound of its geometry, which is $4 \times 10^{-15}$ for a Euclidean double.

DOM-16. Withdrawn on 2026-11-19.

### 12.2. Reference Arithmetic

The reference is the independent high-precision implementation of Section 7 of the architecture, with the precision and tolerances that this section states:

- Structure. A module `reference` in the test binary holds points as arrays of three high-precision numbers and writes each formula componentwise from the research documents. It calls no function of `escher-domain`, `escher-geometry`, or `glam`.
- Independence. The reference builds the standard position from $\cosh L$ and $\sqrt{\cosh^2 L - 1}$ without $\operatorname{arcosh}$, and centres the triangle with $\cos\varphi = c_1/h$, $\sin\varphi = c_2/h$, $\sinh\rho = h$, and $\cosh\rho = c_3$, where $h$ is the length of the first two coordinates of the centre, read from the polar form of Section 8.3 of the geometry reference. The crate takes the other path in both places, so a transcription error is not shared. For a Euclidean domain, the reference computes the vertices of steps 1 to 5 of Section 6.5 componentwise, and the line of each side as $P(a, t)$ of Section 12 of the geometry reference, with $a$ the unit vector perpendicular to the side and $t = a \cdot V$ for an endpoint $V$, without a cross product. For a spherical triangle, the reference builds the standard position from $\cos L$ and $\sqrt{1 - \cos^2 L}$ without $\arccos$, centres it with $\cos\rho = c_3$, $\sin\rho = h$, $\cos\varphi = c_1/h$, and $\sin\varphi = c_2/h$ for the reference centre $c$ of unit length, without $\operatorname{atan2}$. The reference finds each normal by the Gram-Schmidt process, without the cross product of `spherical::wall_through`. For the side from $V_{j-1}$ to $V_j$, it takes $e_1 = V_{j-1} / \lvert V_{j-1} \rvert$ and $e_2$ the unit vector of $V_j - (V_j \cdot e_1) e_1$, removes from the opposite vertex its components along $e_1$ and $e_2$, and takes the negated unit vector of the remainder, so the normal points away from the opposite vertex. The reference side lengths are the arccosines of the three values $X_a$, $X_c$, and $X_b$ of the law of cosines, in the arccosine of the reference type. The reference angles of a triangle are $\pi/n_j$, and its reference area is $\sum_j \pi/n_j - \pi$. For a lune, it computes the vertices and normals of Section 6.7 from the reference values of $\cos(\theta/2)$ and $\sin(\theta/2)$. For a double, it builds its own generating domain as above, reflects the vertices and normals componentwise in the reference wall of side 1 by the reflection formula of its geometry, and multiplies the reference reflections for the pairings, so it shares no code with step 3 of Section 6.8. For the test "Compensated pairings", it also forms the product of the two `f64` reflections that the domain stores in the reference arithmetic, at 256 bits. For a polygon, it follows steps 2 to 9 of Section 6.10 from the same `f64` parameter values, with each gap from $\cosh$ and $\sinh$ of the reference type, the walk with the matrices written componentwise, and the placement from the polar form of the centre, as for the triangle, so it shares the formulas of the steps and none of their code. It also builds the inscribed polygon of DOM-45 with its own bisection to $10^{-70}$, and finds the distance from a point to a side by a golden-section search over the side. For the test "Walk against weld", it lays out each sheet by a frame that turns at each corner and moves along each length and gap. Each weld is the orientation-preserving isometry that takes the frame at the first end of the slice in the cut sheet to the frame at its second end in the sheet that holds the slice, followed by a half-turn. It leaves out SymmHub's splice lists and recentrings. The distance between two walls is the smallest distance between a point of one wall and a point of the other, which uses only the distance between points and not the relation of step 4 of DOM-45. A golden-section search of 170 steps runs over the points $p(s) = m_a + s e_a$ of the first wall, for the projective parameter $s$ in $(-1, 1)$. For each $p(s)$, the reference takes the square of the smallest hyperbolic cosine of the distance to the points $m_b + t e_b$ of the second wall in closed form, as $(P^2 - Q^2)/(1 - s^2)$ with $P = -\langle p, m_b \rangle$ and $Q = -\langle p, e_b \rangle$, from the minimum of $(P + Q t)/\sqrt{1 - t^2}$ over $t$ (Section 14). A nested golden-section search over both walls, with 130 steps on each level, took 167 s for the test "Cut meaning" and reached $8.9 \times 10^{-51}$ against the tolerance $10^{-50}$, a margin of 1.1, at `*(1000)2222222`. The search with the closed form reaches $5.2 \times 10^{-55}$ in about 16 s. For one of the ten domains of Section 6.13, the reference computes $w$, $h$, and the vertices of the table of DOM-50 from `aspect` and `skew` in its own arithmetic, applies the shear to the vertices of `2222` componentwise, takes the line of each side as $P(a, t)$ as for a Euclidean domain, and writes each pairing as a matrix from its definition: a translation by its vector, a glide reflection as the reflection in its axis followed by the translation along it, and a half-turn about the point $c$ as the map $x \mapsto 2c - x$. For a kite, it builds the reference kite of `442` or `632` as for a double. It shares no code with Section 6.13. For a spherical kite, it builds the reference double of `432`, `(2n)22`, or `n22` as above and writes the reflection of each mirror side from its reference normal by the reflection formula of the sphere. For the domain of `nx`, it builds the reference lune and writes each pairing as $\operatorname{diag}(-1, -1, 1)$ times the reference reflection, componentwise. The lune of order 1 and the whole sphere need no reference, because their values are exact. It shares no code with Section 6.16.
- Precision. 256 bits, rounding to nearest with ties to even.
- Comparison. As in Section 10.2 of the geometry design, each test converts the `f64` result exactly into the reference type, takes the difference there, and compares it with the tolerance there, because `astro-float-num` has no public conversion back to `f64`. A message that prints a measured error formats the number as decimal text.
- Self-check. The test "Reference self-check" in `reference` checks the reference library itself: $\pi$ against a literal with 80 digits after the decimal point, $\cos(\pi/3) = 1/2$, $\cosh(\operatorname{arcosh} 2) = 2$, and $\sqrt{2}^2 = 2$, each to $10^{-70}$. The largest of the four errors is $1.7 \times 10^{-77}$. It guards against a wrong precision or rounding argument and does not close `UNV-25`.

DOM-15. The reference module MUST share no code with `escher-domain` or `escher-geometry`, because a formula that the crate and its test share passes the test whether or not it is correct (Section 7 of the architecture).

## 13. Dependencies

DOM-17. The crate MUST declare only the dependencies of this section, each from one entry in the `[workspace.dependencies]` table of the root `Cargo.toml`, because each further dependency adds build time, licence obligations, and supply-chain risk, and a version declared twice can diverge (DEP-2 of the coding standard).

| Dependency | Kind | Features | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| `escher-signature` | Normal, with a version on the path dependency (TOOL-25 of `docs/design/tooling.md`) | None | The input type `Signature` and the `Geometry` that `geometry()` returns |
| `escher-geometry` | Normal, with a version on the path dependency (TOOL-25) | None | The linear model (Section 3.1 of the architecture) |
| `escher-group` | Normal, public, with a version on the path dependency (TOOL-25) | None | The type `Presentation` that `side_presentation()` returns, and `enumerate` in the tests of Section 12.1 |
| `glam` | Normal, public | The workspace entry that the geometry design states, which provides `DVec3` and `DMat3` | The types of the public surface (`DEC-4`) |
| `thiserror` | Normal | Default | Derives the `Display` of the private detail of the error (Section 10.1) |
| `proptest` | Dev | The workspace entry | The property tests |
| `astro-float-num` | Dev | `default-features = false`, with `std` | The reference arithmetic (Section 12.2) |

`escher-group` depends on `escher-signature` only, so the graph has no cycle (Section 3.3 of the architecture). `Presentation` appears in a public signature of this crate, so DEP-3 of the coding standard makes `escher-group` a public dependency: a breaking change of `escher-group` is a breaking change of this crate. Both crates set `publish = false`, so the cost stays inside the workspace.

`glam` types appear in the public signatures of this crate, so DEP-3 of the coding standard makes `glam` a public dependency, with the obligations that Section 3.5 of the architecture states: its version is part of the version of this crate, the crate stays below 1.0 while `glam` does, and a breaking upgrade of `glam` is a breaking change of the crate.

The reference crate departs from `DEC-3`, which names `astro-float`, for the reason and with the resolution facts that Section 11 of the geometry design records. In short, `astro-float` brings `syn` 1 beside the locked `syn` 3 and fails the duplicate check of `cargo deny`, while `astro-float-num` passes. Section 15.3 asks the owner to confirm the change.

The crate sets `publish = false` (`DEC-22`), inherits the workspace fields and lints, and declares no Cargo feature.

Its index 2 is sourced (Section 9.3 of the notation reference) for the hyperbolic and Euclidean reflection groups, and the orders of Section 8.2 of the notation reference give the ratio 2 for the spherical ones | The test asserts the index only. A source or a Lean proof would close it |
| `UNV-34` | The domains of `3*2`, `2*n`, `n*`, `nx`, `x`, `*`, and `1` with their pairings and vertex cycles (DOM-58 and DOM-59), that the group that their pairings generate has the signature of the domain, the walk of DOM-55 on a lune with reversing pairings, and the values of the census (DOM-64), none of which a source states (items 19 and 20 of Section 13.3 of the domain reference) | The guard of DOM-60 verifies every built domain other than that of `*`, so a wrong construction gives `Numerical` and not a wrong domain, and the tests of DOM-64 check the order, the census, and the exactly-once tiling. None closes it. A source that states each domain with its pairings, or a Lean proof, would |
| `UNV-29` and `UNV-30` | That a lune is a cell of the polygon theorems, for the domains of `nx`, `x`, and `*` | As for the lunes of `*nn` and `nn`: the certificates of DOM-64 enumerate each group and test the exactly-once tiling |
| `UNV-31` | That Definition 2.8 of [EPSTEIN-PETRONIO1994] admits a mirror side split at a flat vertex, for the two mirror sides of `n*` and the sides of `x` and `*` | Check 3 tests each half against its endpoints, check 4 walks the flat corner, and the certificate tests the tiling |
| `UNV-20` | The text `1` of the trivial signature, which `escher-signature` parses and this crate builds as the whole sphere | The tests of the whole sphere of Section 12.1 assert its domain and its presentation, and not the reading of the text |
| None | That every orientation-reversing isometry of the sphere is a rotary reflection, with the antipodal map as its case of the angle $\pi$, which the kind `RotaryReflection` relies on (item 33 of Section 15.1 of the geometry reference) | The kind is a label that the renderer never reads. Check 2 ties it to the orientation, the determinant, and the plane (DOM-61). A source that states it in words, or a Lean proof, would close it |
| `UNV-1` and `UNV-2` | The termination of the fold of `escher-render` on the spherical group domains | This crate does not fold, except in the certificate tests, which assert the fold of their sample points only. `docs/design/render.md` owns the fold and its bounds |

The hyperbolic construction does not rely on `UNV-28`, because it uses SymmHub's triangle neither in the code nor as a test oracle (Section 6.3). `UNV-4` concerns the precision of the disk map near the limit circle, and the crate maps to the disk only in the test "Circumradius of $\{7, 3\}$", at a point near the origin.

These statements of this design rest on figures that no measurement of the code covers. They come from a double-precision model of the algorithm outside the repository, and each supports only the choice that it names:

| Statement | Section | Figures of the model |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A domain left with vertex 2 at the origin has larger normal components and fails checks that the final position passes | 6.3 | At $(1000, 3, 2)$, a largest normal component of 1 and a relator residual of $7.28 \times 10^{-12}$, which passes. At $(1000, 100, 100)$, 63.6 and $6.23 \times 10^{-4}$, which fails. At $(1000, 1000, 1000)$, 637 and an infinite residual. In the family $(n, n, n)$, $(60, 60, 60)$ and $(70, 70, 70)$ fail checks 1 and 2, $(80, 80, 80)$ also fails check 4, and every larger order fails. The families $(n, 3, 2)$, $(n, 4, 4)$, and $(n, n, 2)$ fail no check up to $n = 1000$ |
| Repeated multiplication evaluates the relator more accurately than repeated squaring | 8.2 | At corner 0 of $(1000, 3, 2)$, a largest entry of $C^{1000} - I$ of $3.46 \times 10^{-9}$ in the domain frame by repeated multiplication and $7.77 \times 10^{-8}$ by repeated squaring, against $2.33 \times 10^{-11}$ in the corner frame by either method |
| The residual of check 6 stays close to that of check 5 | 8.2 | Never more than 2.91 times the residual of check 5, over 300 random triples |
| Renormalizing the vertices after step 8 makes them less accurate (DOM-10) | 9.1 | At $(1000, 1000, 1000)$, renormalization raised the largest relative vertex error from $5.7 \times 10^{-11}$ to $8.9 \times 10^{-6}$ and the angle error from $1.9 \times 10^{-13}$ to $2.8 \times 10^{-8}$ |

A second prototype outside the repository built the doubles of Section 6.8 from the public values of the reflection domains of this crate, with plain double-precision products as pairings except where a row names rounded products, which it computed in 256-bit arithmetic from the exact values of the two stored reflections and rounded once to the nearest `f64`, and copied the fold of `escher-render`. It checked its copies of `verify()` and of the fold against those of the crates, bit for bit, on the reflection domains. These statements of this design rest on its figures, which no measurement of the code covers. Each figure was measured on that prototype, and each supports only the choice that it names:

| Statement | Section | Figures of the prototype |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Side 1 is the wall to double | 6.8 | The double of `*(1000)32` across side 2 fails check 4 with the residual $2.42 \times 10^{-6}$ against the tolerance $2 \times 10^{-6}$, and 338 of its folds do not converge in band 6 of Section 6.4 of `docs/design/render.md` |
| The pairings are compensated products (DOM-25) | 6.8 | The plain products of the double of `(1000)32` have isometry defects up to $2.7 \times 10^{-12}$, and one fold of its fold set within the range of accuracy does not converge. With the rounded products, no fold of the fold set fails to converge |
| The six checks keep their tolerances for the kites of every hyperbolic triple | 8.4 | Over all 166,665,495 hyperbolic triples: isometries and pairings $2.547 \times 10^{-11}$ at `(992)63`, a margin of 39.3, walls $8.719 \times 10^{-15}$ at `(952)53`, relators, angles, and area as over the cone triples of the set of 294 (Section 8.4), and no failure |
| The spherical doubles keep the tolerances at orders outside the set of Section 9.6 | 8.4 | Over 37 spherical doubles: isometries $2.0 \times 10^{-15}$ at `(10)22`, relators $3.1 \times 10^{-14}$ at `(999)22`, angles $7.06 \times 10^{-14}$ at `(998)22` and `(1000)22`, and area $4.24 \times 10^{-14}$ at `(998)(998)` |
| The vertices of a double lie within the tolerance of the fold | 15.3 | The largest ratio of the test "Vertices" of Section 6.2 of `docs/design/render.md` is $3.22 \times 10^{-16}$ over its named sets, a margin of 12.4, and $4.11 \times 10^{-16}$ over the cone triples of the set of 294, a margin of 9.7. Over all 166,665,495 hyperbolic triples it is $1.224 \times 10^{-15}$ at `(868)(416)5`, a margin of 3.27, and 680,942 doubles exceed one eighth of the tolerance $4 \times 10^{-15}$. No vertex of any double exceeds the tolerance |

The tests of `escher-render` cover two figures of this table with the domains that this crate builds: every fold of the rotation fold sets, including that of `(1000)32`, converges with the compensated products, and the largest ratio at a vertex of `(868)(416)5` is the same as on the prototype (Sections 6.2 and 6.8 of `docs/design/render.md`). The measurements of the crate in Sections 8.4, 9.6, and 12.1 cover the other figures of this prototype: the six checks of the kites over the cone triples of the set of 294, the cycles of two vertices, the Euclidean doubles, the spherical doubles of Section 9.6, the angle of a flat vertex, the effect of the convexity term on the reflection domains (DOM-30), and the fold steps of the certificate tests. The largest residual of check 4 over the cycles of two vertices is $1.094 \times 10^{-8}$, at `(973)(972)(964)`, where the prototype reported at most $1.024 \times 10^{-8}$, at `(995)(987)(976)`.

A third prototype outside the repository built the side presentations of Section 5.4 from the public values of the domains of this crate, with each corner order recovered from the corner angles, enumerated them with its own implementation of the enumeration of Section 7 of the group design, and evaluated their relators. Each figure was measured on that prototype, and each supports only the choice that it names:

| Statement | Section | Figures of the prototype |
| --- | --- | --- |
| The side presentations give the group order on the sphere (test "Side orders") | 5.4, 12.1 | The 52 spherical domains of the set of "Side orders" other than `*(1000)(1000)` and `(1000)(1000)`, which the prototype did not run, give `group_order()`, and each enumeration defines exactly that many cosets. `*(1000)22` takes 83 ms in a release build and 309 ms in a debug build |
| The relators evaluate to the identity (test "Relators evaluate") | 12.1 | Largest residual $6 \times 10^{-15}$ at orders up to 12, with exactly 0 for `*732`, `2222`, and `*2222`, and $2.7 \times 10^{-11}$ at the spherical order 1000 |
| No built domain fixes the convention (DOM-36) | 5.4, 12.1 | Every vertex cycle of every built double visits one or two sides, and the reversed bases and the opposite convention give residuals equal to those of DOM-32 |
| The hyperbolic relators need the corner frame (DOM-36) | 12.1 | In the domain frame, the residual reaches 3.3 for `(1000)(1000)(1000)` and $4 \times 10^{-2}$ for `*(1000)(1000)(1000)`, where check 4 reports $5.6 \times 10^{-8}$ |

The tests of the crate cover the first two rows (Section 12.1). "Side orders" also runs `*(1000)(1000)` and `(1000)(1000)`, which the prototype did not run. The second row predates the wallpaper domains and covers only the orders up to 12 and the spherical order 1000. The table below DOM-36 records no residual of the wallpaper domains, and the fifth prototype below gives only its own figure for them. The largest residuals of the crate are at the hyperbolic triples near the limit, which the table below DOM-36 records, and each is within its tolerance. No measurement of the crate covers the third row, because no test asserts the residual of a reversed base or of the opposite convention on a built domain, or the fourth row, because "Relators evaluate" evaluates only in the corner frame.

A fourth prototype outside the repository tested the polygon construction in two parts. In the first, a copy of the library crates of the workspace gained one module that built the polygon by the steps of a draft of Section 6.10, with a plain `f64` walk and variants, and ran the real `verify()`, `side_presentation()`, fold, renderer, and colouring search on it. In the second, a Python program with mpmath 1.4.1, at 50 to 120 digits, gave the exact polygon, placed as step 8 places it and rounded once to `f64`, which the rows call the exact polygon rounded once, and simulated the walk at 106 bits. The prototype did not implement double-double arithmetic. A margin is the tolerance divided by the residual. The default sweep is the set of signatures on which the prototype built the rounded default shapes. It holds the canonical signatures with four to eight corners and orders up to 12, among them `*99994`, `*(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)(12)`, and `*(12)(12)333333`, and the signatures with a corner of order 1000: 28 with four corners, 73 with five, 208 with six, 586 with seven, and 1720 with eight. Its report does not list the signatures with orders up to 12 in full, and Section 9.7 states the sweep as the crate ran it from the code of the prototype. Each figure was measured on that prototype, and each supports only the choice that it names:

| --- | --- | --- |

At 120 digits with lengths from $10^{-6}$ to 60, every polygon convex with the right angles to about $10^{-33}$ |
| The cut sheets are recorded as $(y, x, \bar s)$ (DOM-39) | 6.10 | In the order $(x, y, s)$, closure errors of 9.2 for `*3333`, 2.0 for `*5432`, 2.2 for `*22222`, and 7.8 for `*65432`. In the order of DOM-39, every walk closed |

| The walk is in double-double arithmetic (DOM-42) | 6.10, 6.12 | The figures of the plain `f64` walk of Section 6.12. With 106 bits, check 5 of `*4444444` with every length 1 went from $7 \times 10^{-10}$ to $4 \times 10^{-15}$, check 5 at the default of `*(1000)(1000)(1000)(1000)` from $4 \times 10^{-7}$ to $9 \times 10^{-14}$, and the default of 12 corners of order 1000 had a margin of 45 |

The report states that with the exact polygon rounded once or the 106-bit walk, every signature of the default sweep had a margin of at least 17 at its default shape. At the rounded defaults of the crate, the exact construction of the prototype gives margins below 8 for some signatures with seven and eight corners (Section 9.7) |

The walk rebuilt the inscribed polygon from the unrounded defaults to $10^{-25}$ or better. `*3333` gave 1.3170 and `*22222` 1.0613. Over the default sweep, the closest unrounded default lay $9.3 \times 10^{-9}$ from a boundary of the rounding, at `*432232`, and the defaults lay from 0.658 to 3.06, and the crate measures 3.23 as the largest over the same sweep (Section 6.6) |

The doubles had their centres within $9.9 \times 10^{-14}$ of the origin, and their angles at the vertices with the copies 2 within $1.2 \times 10^{-10}$ by the sum of two angles from the law of cosines and $6.4 \times 10^{-11}$ by the corner frame |

The prototype also ran the real fold of `escher-render` on four polygons at the default and extreme shapes, and the colouring search of `escher-group` on 17 polygons and doubles. Those figures belong to `docs/design/render.md` and `docs/design/cli.md`.

Measurements of the crate cover several rows of this table in part. The comparison of raw bits below DOM-30 (Section 8.4) covers the row of the triangles and kites. The tests "Doubled polygon measurements" and "Flat vertex" cover the angles at the vertices with the copies 2 in the row of the corner frame, on regular polygons built through private access (Section 9.7). The measurements of Section 9.7 cover the rows of the refusal rates, of the limit of eight corners, and of the double-double walk at the default shapes, and the defaults of Section 6.6 cover part of the row of the default shape. In 256-bit arithmetic, the tests "Polygons", "Cut meaning", "Walk against weld", and the reference part of "Default shape" cover the rows of the construction, of the meaning of a slice length, and of the default shape, and the test "Rotation polygons" covers the centres of the doubles in the row of the corner frame (Section 9.7). The sweeps of the doubles of Section 8.4 cover the row of the doubles of polygons. Few doubles with an order of 1000 fail there, which contradicts that part of the row.

A fifth prototype outside the repository tested the design of the ten domains of Section 6.13. It built the domains through a private function and not through `build_with`, so it did not run the guard, `UnknownParameter`, or `ParameterOutOfRange` for `skew`. A sixth prototype outside the repository tested the design of the spherical group domains of Section 6.16. It was a copy of the six crates with the smallest patches to `escher-domain`: the predicate of the fourth class with the limits, the arm of `build_with` and the guard, `PairingKind::RotaryReflection` with its arms, the arms of the parameters, the two changes of DOM-62, the lune of order 1 with exact normals, the plane rule of check 2, and `Side::spherical_mirror` made `pub(crate)`. It kept the name `Closure::Wallpaper` and built its kites with a builder of its own of about 20 lines, which took the double, the mirror pair, and the corners. It ran the real `verify()`, `side_presentation()`, `enumerate`, `count_colourings`, `colourings`, `fold`, `Renderer`, `CosetFill`, and the binary `escher`, with no change to `escher-render`, `escher-group`, `escher-geometry`, `escher-signature`, or `escher-cli`. It compared the stored data with an independent construction in 200-bit arithmetic of mpmath. Where the summary of its report and one of its own tables disagree, this design takes the table. The tests of Section 12.1 cover these figures on the crate, except the comparison with SymmHub, the domains built past the limit of DOM-63, and the counts beyond 16 colours, which no test repeats. The prototype also measured the fold, the images, the byte equalities, and the colouring ids of the spherical group domains. Those figures belong to `docs/design/render.md` and `docs/design/cli.md`.

## 15. Assumptions, Choices, and Points for the Owner

### 15.1. Assumptions

These readings of this design rest on no sentence of a source. Each names what checks it:

- The triangle placed by the two sides $L_a$ and $L_b$ with the included angle $\alpha_2$ is the triangle with the angles $\alpha_0$, $\alpha_1$, and $\alpha_2$. The research states uniqueness given the three angles (Section 10.1 of the geometry reference), but no statement on two sides and the included angle. The test "Third side" and checks 4 and 5 check it. The same reading applies to the spherical triangle of Section 6.7, which checks 4 and 5 and the test "Spherical domains" check.
- The angle at a vertex is the arccosine of $(\cosh a \cosh b - \cosh c) / (\sinh a \sinh b)$. This is the first law of cosines of Section 10.2 of the geometry reference solved for the angle, which no source prints in this form. The test "Triangles" compares the angles with $\pi/n_j$ in the reference.
- The normalized sum of the vertices lies strictly inside the triangle, hyperbolic or spherical. Check 2 fails if it does not, and the tests "Random triples" and "Spherical table" check it.
- A power of a matrix is the identity exactly when the same power of its conjugate is. This is matrix algebra, not a statement of the geometry reference. The test "Relator agreement" checks it on the 30 named triples.
- Side 0, side 1, and side 2 are the Latin reflections $P$, $Q$, and $R$ of step 3 of Section 9.1 of the notation reference, and its fourth reflection $S$ is $P$ again for a triangle and side 3 for the rectangle. The relators that the checks use come from Section 4.4 of the domain reference and do not depend on this reading.
- `hyperbolic::translation(c)` maps `hyperbolic::ORIGIN` to the point with the spatial coordinates of $c$, as Section 6.4 of the geometry design states, and this is $c$ itself only when $c$ lies on the hyperboloid. Section 13.1 of the geometry design records this property as an assumption and names the test that checks it.
- The angle of a kite at $W_0$ or $W_2$ is the sum of the angles of its two triangles there, and its area is the sum of their areas (DOM-29). The test "Rotation domains" compares the angles and the area with the reference, and check 5 compares each angle with the copies times $\pi$ divided by the order.
- The image of a wall under $R_1$ has the normal $R_1 u$ for the normal $u$, which is outward for the image side, and in the Euclidean plane the covector $R_1^{T} c$ for the covector $c$ (step 3 of Section 6.8). No source prints these forms. Checks 2 and 3 test each image wall against its vertices and the centre, and the test "Rotation domains" compares it with the reference, which reflects the normals componentwise.
- The centre of the generating domain lies strictly inside its double. Check 2 fails if it does not.
- For the unit normals $u_0$ and $u_1$ of the double of a lune, $\operatorname{atan2}(\lvert u_0 \times u_1 \rvert, -u_0 \cdot u_1)$ is the same angle as $\arccos(-u_0 \cdot u_1)$ (DOM-20). No source read states this form. Checks 4 and 5, the test "Rotation domains", which compares each angle with the copies times $\pi$ divided by the order, and the exhaustive certificates check it. A source that states it, or a Lean proof, would close it.
- The wall term of check 2, $P_i^{T} c_p + c_i$, is 0 when $P_i$ carries the wall of side $i$ onto the wall of its partner $p$ with the domain on opposite sides (Section 8.2). No source read states this form. The defect of the half-turn on `44` fails check 2 through this term alone, and every double of Section 9.6 passes it. A source that states it, or a Lean proof, would close it.
- The compensated product of DOM-25 is correctly rounded for every entry of every pairing of a double other than a double of a polygon. The test "Compensated pairings" asserts it for the doubles of Section 9.6 only. For the doubles of polygons it does not hold: 2 of the 1692 entries of the 20 named doubles of that measurement are one unit in the last place from the nearest `f64` (Section 6.8), and the test "Rotation polygons" asserts only a bound on their error. Compensated arithmetic does not guarantee correct rounding in general, and no test covers the other doubles, such as the kites of the cone triples outside the set of 294 triples, or `2222` at other values of `aspect`. A proof of correct rounding for the entries of products of two reflections, or a test over every supported double, would close it.
- A canonical rotation signature lists its cone orders in descending order, so the orders of a rotation signature are the corner orders of its starred signature in the same order (Section 4). The test "Rotation table" checks it through the spellings `237` and `22n`. For four or more orders, the starred signature of `p1...pn` is the star followed by its cone orders in descending order, and a descending sequence of corners is its own canonical form under step 4 of Section 7.2 of the signature design, so `3322` gives `*3322` and `5432` gives `*5432`. The test "Rotation polygons" checks that the starred signature of each double is canonical.
- In the corner frame of a vertex, the angle between the first two coordinates of its two neighbours is the hyperbolic angle at the vertex (DOM-46). This reads the conformality of the disk at its origin and its diameters as geodesics ([CFKP], p. 80) through the disk map, which keeps the direction of the first two coordinates. No source states it for the hyperboloid. The tests "Polygons" and "Rotation polygons" compare each angle with the reference, and check 4 tests the same corners from the pairings.
- The area of a double with a flat vertex is $(m - 2)\pi$ minus its angle sum with that vertex counted with the angle $\pi$. Theorem 7.15.1 of [BEA] states the area of a polygon, and a vertex of the angle $\pi$ is not a vertex of a polygon in its sense. The test "Rotation polygons" compares the area with the area of the signature.
- A closed walk whose vertices all lie inside or on every wall of its sides, with angles in $(0, \pi]$ and an angle sum below $(m - 2)\pi$, bounds a simple convex polygon. The guard reads checks 3 and 5 this way (Section 6.11). The test "Polygons" compares every vertex with the reference, whose polygon is convex by construction.
- The rounding of a default to three decimals moves the polygon by less than the margins of its checks. The test "Checks at the worst cases" builds every default and checks it.
- The closed form of the translation in step 8 of Section 6.10 equals the product $R(\varphi) \, B(\rho) \, R(-\varphi)$ of `hyperbolic::translation`, and its inverse is that matrix with the entries that mix the time coordinate with a space coordinate negated. This is matrix algebra, which no source read prints in this form. The guard checks every placed polygon, and the test "Cyclic order" checks that the centre lies within $10^{-9}$ of the origin and vertex 0 on the positive first axis.
- A half of a split side of `22*` or `2*22` lies on the wall of the side of $F$ that it halves, so it can take that covector bit for bit (step 4 of Section 6.13). Check 3 tests each half against its endpoints.
- The shear $(x, y) \mapsto (x + k a y, y)$ keeps the horizontal sides of `2222` horizontal, so its sheared half-turns are half-turns about the sheared centres, and its flat vertices stay flat. No source read states it for this domain. Checks 2, 4, and 5 test the pairings, the cycles, and the angles on every build, and the tests measure every flat vertex at exactly $\pi$ (Section 6.13).
- The ranges of `skew` reach every lattice of the family at a given `aspect`, and both ends give the same rhombic lattice (Section 6.6). This is part of `UNV-33`, and no test checks it.
- The angle of a vertex of a wallpaper domain is the Euclidean angle of Section 7 between its two neighbours, and the angle sum over the states of a cycle counts a vertex once for each state. Check 5 reads it so (DOM-56), and the test "Wallpaper accuracy" compares the sums with $2\pi/m_c$ in the reference.
- The product $H R_i$ of the half-turn $H = \operatorname{diag}(-1, -1, 1)$ and the reflection of side $i$ of the lune of `*nn` is the pairing of `nx` that maps the neighbouring copy across side $i$ onto the lune (DOM-59). No source states it. Check 2 tests it on every build, and the certificate of DOM-64 tests the group.
- The angle of the lune of `nx` and of `x` is the angle of the lune of `*nn` with the same normals, by the arccosine of DOM-20. Checks 4 and 5 and the certificate test it.
- The two mirror sides of `n*` lie on one great circle up to rounding, and the flat vertex between them can sit at any point of that circle between the two neighbouring vertices (Section 6.16). Check 3 tests each side against its endpoints, and the test "Flat vertex of `n*`" records that `verify()` does not fix the point.
- The whole sphere with no side satisfies the hypotheses of the polygon theorems vacuously, so it is a fundamental domain of the trivial group (DOM-62). No source states it for a domain with no side. The certificate checks that its closure has one element and that every sample point lies in it.
- The kite of `n22` with the mirrors on sides 2 and 3 is the domain of `n*`, and the kite of `(2n)22` with the mirrors on sides 0 and 1 the domain of `2*n`, and not the other way round. The census of DOM-64 tells them apart, and the test "Census defects" builds the swapped domains.

### 15.2. Choices of This Design

No other document dictates these choices:

| `Orientation` with both variants, and `PairingKind` with five (item 20 of Section 15.3 for `Rotation` and `Translation`) | 3.2 |
| The domain stores the geometry and the corner orders of its signature, the area of a hyperbolic or spherical signature, and its vertices, sides, and centre, although all of them follow from the signature. They are the result of the construction, which the renderer would otherwise repeat, and the type is immutable, so they cannot diverge. It stores no signature, because `build` borrows it and the checks need only these values. A reviewer checks this against TYPE-3 of the coding standard | 3.2, 6.1 |
| The area of the signature converted to `f64` once, at construction, with no limit on the size of its numerator or denominator | 6.1 |
| Vertex $j$ is corner $j$, between side $j$ and side $j + 1$, and the vertices are counterclockwise | 5.1, 5.2 |
