### 15.3. Points for the Owner

None of these blocks the crate. Each states what the design does until the owner decides:

1. Reference crate. `DEC-3` names `astro-float`. The design uses `astro-float-num` (Section 13). The alternative is a `skip` entry in `deny.toml` for `syn` 1 with a reason. Section 13.3 of the geometry design raises the same point.
2. Largest order and the tolerance of check 4. The design supports corner orders up to 1000 and returns `LimitExceeded` above it (DOM-3). It sets the tolerance of check 4 to $2 \times 10^{-6}$, because the largest residual of that check would leave a margin of 7.5 at $10^{-6}$, below the 8 that TEST-13 of the coding standard requires, and the margin at $2 \times 10^{-6}$ is 15.0, with check 4 still failing every triangle near the limit with one corner order off by one (Sections 8.3 and 8.4). A higher limit needs a construction of the far walls that is better conditioned, which the research does not source, and new measurements of every margin.
3. Comparison with MPFR for `UNV-25`. The geometry design records the comparison (its Section 12) and asks whether it closes `UNV-25`. This design relies on the same answer.
4. Check 6. It detects nothing that check 5 does not, apart from a disagreement with `escher-signature` (Section 8.2). The design keeps it because the plan requires an area check. The alternatives are to drop it, or to measure the area by an independent formula from the vertices, which needs a source that the research does not hold. For a polygon, its residual is the sum of the angle errors, at most $m$ times the residual of check 5, and on a prototype it was the check that failed first in most refusals (Section 8.2). The design keeps its tolerance of $10^{-9}$ for polygons. A further alternative is the tolerance $m \cdot 10^{-9}$, which would refuse fewer settings and would make check 6 depend on the number of sides.
5. Placement. The final position makes the centre of the canonical view a property of the domain, while Section 3.2 of the architecture places the canonical placement in `View`. `View` can still move the domain, because `centre()` is public.
6. Vertices of the Euclidean triangle. No source gives them, so the design builds the triangle from the candidate of `UNV-28` and certifies each build by checks 3 and 5 and the test "SymmHub lines" (Section 6.5). The alternative is to build only `*2222` until a source or a Lean proof closes the candidate.
7. Units of the Euclidean domains. The triangle has side 0 of length 1, and the rectangle has the area 1 (Section 6.5), so `aspect` and its reciprocal give the same rectangle turned a quarter turn. This does not hold for the double of `2222`, whose doubled wall is always the right side of its generating rectangle (item 24). The alternative for the rectangle is the height 1 and the width `aspect`, which makes the size of a tile depend on `aspect`.
8. Range and default of `aspect`. The design takes 0.125 to 8 with the default 1, the square (Section 6.6). Any other closed range of positive values that contains 1 is an alternative, and no source gives one.
9. The area of a Euclidean domain. `area()` uses a formula that no source states (Section 7, item 25 of Section 15.1 of the geometry reference). The alternative is to give no area for a Euclidean domain, which removes the line `Domain area` that `escher info` prints for it.
10. Check 6 for a Euclidean domain. The design keeps the angle defect, which fails among the defects of Section 8.3 only for a NaN vertex, so that the six checks exist for every geometry (Section 8.2). The alternative is to compare the measured area with the area that the unit of item 7 implies, which would detect a wrong scale but rests on the area formula of item 9.
11. `build_with` beside `build`. The design adds a second constructor that takes the settings, so that the callers of `build` do not change (Section 3.4). The alternative is to give `build` a second argument and change every call.
12. Validation in `escher-domain`. The crate reports an unknown name and a value out of range (DOM-18), and a caller detects only a name set twice, through the return value of `ShapeParameters::set`. The alternative is to validate in `escher-cli`, which would duplicate the ranges.
13. The spherical domains. The design builds `*332`, `*432`, `*532`, `*222`, `*n22`, and the lune of `*nn` with $n$ up to the limit of 1000, which includes `*22`, with no new public item and no shape parameter (Sections 4 and 6.7). The alternatives are to leave `*22` with the other signatures of two corners of order 2 for a later milestone, or a lower limit for the spherical families, whose tiles at $n = 1000$ are smaller than a pixel of a default image.
14. The lune on an unverified statement. No source makes the lune a fundamental domain, so the design builds it on `UNV-29` and certifies it by the tests of DOM-22 (Section 6.7). The alternative is to build no lune until a source or a Lean proof closes the statement, which leaves `*nn` unsupported.
15. The placement of the lune. The design places the lune directly, with its vertices on the second axis and its centre at the origin (Section 6.7), because the rule of `DEC-12` gives no centre for two antipodal vertices. The alternative is a centre at the midpoint of one side, which the view would not centre on the domain.
16. The angle of the lune from its normals. DOM-20 makes it the one exception to DOM-6, because the vertices do not determine the sides. The alternative is to measure no angle for a lune and to drop check 5 for it, which leaves the angle to check 4 and the certificate alone.
17. The readings of the checks and their precondition for a spherical domain. The design keeps the six checks and reads each per geometry (Section 8.2), with a vertex that is finite and not the zero vector (DOM-21). The alternative is a separate set of checks for the sphere, which `escher info --check` would print differently.
18. The tolerances and the limit. The design keeps every tolerance of Section 8.4 and the limit of 1000 for the spherical domains, and sets the bounds of Section 9.5 from the largest errors measured. Every check passes on the spherical domains of Section 9.5 with a margin of at least $2.45 \times 10^{4}$. The alternative is tolerances of their own for the sphere.
19. The doubled wall. The design doubles across side 1, the wall through $V_0$ and $V_1$ (DOM-23, Section 6.8), because on a prototype the double of `*(1000)32` across side 2 failed check 4 and left folds unconverged (Section 14). The alternative is side 2, the wall opposite the corner of the largest order.
20. The kinds of a pairing. The design adds the kinds `Rotation` and `Translation` (Section 3.2). The alternative is one kind for every orientation-preserving pairing, which would call the translation of the double of `2222`, and the translations of the double of a polygon whose walls do not meet the doubled wall, rotations.
21. The accessor `reverses_orientation()`. The design adds it, so that `escher-cli` decides in one place whether a parity colouring exists (Section 3.2). The alternative is that each caller scans `sides()`.
22. The corner of a vertex. The design replaces the stored corner orders with a private corner that holds the order and the copies (Section 3.2). The alternative is to store the expected angle of each vertex, which check 4 would then have to turn back into an order.
23. The pairings of a double as compensated products (DOM-25). The design computes each pairing with compensated arithmetic at construction, because the plain product left one fold of `(1000)32` unconverged within the range of accuracy on a prototype (Section 14). The test "Compensated pairings" asserts that every entry of the compensated products of the doubles of Section 9.6 is correctly rounded, while the plain products misround entries in every geometry. Compensated arithmetic does not guarantee correct rounding for the other doubles, which Section 15.1 records. Section 9.6 also records that the compensated product does not lower the largest isometry defect over the cone triples of the 30 named triples: it lowers the defect at some doubles, such as `(1000)32`, and raises it at others, such as `(868)44`. The research documents cite no source for the compensated dot product, which is a numerical technique and not a statement of the geometry. The alternatives are plain products with a narrower range of accuracy for the orders near 1000, which `docs/design/render.md` would state, or a fold with the reflections of the generating domain that applies the reflection in the doubled wall when the parity is odd, which needs no rotation pairing in the fold.
24. `aspect` of `2222`. The design makes it the aspect of the generating rectangle, with the area 1, so the double has the area 2 and is twice as wide (Section 6.6). The alternative is a double with the area 1 and `aspect` as the ratio of its own sides, which would change the generating rectangle of `2222` away from that of `*2222` with the same setting.
25. The convexity term of check 3. The design evaluates it for every domain (Section 8.2), and it leaves the residuals of the reflection domains unchanged (DOM-30). The alternative is to evaluate it for the doubles only.
26. The double of the lune on an unverified statement. No source makes it a fundamental domain, so the design builds it on `UNV-30` and certifies it by the tests of DOM-31. The alternative is to leave `nn` unsupported until a source or a Lean proof closes the statement.
27. The margin of the vertices of a double against the tolerance of the fold. On a prototype, no vertex of a double exceeded the tolerance of the fold over every hyperbolic triple, but the margin over that sweep fell below the factor 8 of TEST-13 of the coding standard, while the named sets stayed above it (Section 14 gives the figures). `docs/design/render.md` owns the tolerance and the test "Vertices", and the tolerance cannot rise without changing the images of the reflection domains. The options are to accept the measured margin over every triple, to restrict the claim of the margin to the named sets, or to compute the image vertices and normals as correctly rounded products too, whose effect on the fold no prototype or test measured. The doubles of polygons that the guard refuses fail through their stored pairings, not their image vertices (Section 8.4), and no measurement covers the effect of such products on their check 4 (Section 6.8).
28. The tolerances of the doubles. The design keeps every tolerance of Section 8.4 and the limit of 1000 for the doubles, and sets the bounds of Section 9.6 from the largest errors measured. Every check passes on the doubles of Section 9.6 with a margin of at least 15.0 (Section 8.4). The alternative is tolerances of their own for the doubles.
29. One generator for each side (`DEC-36` of the architecture). The side presentation keeps both generators of a pair with the pair relator (Section 5.4), so a colouring finds the generator of a fold step by its side index. The alternative is one generator for each pair, which halves the generators of a double and the columns of its coset tables, and needs a map from a side to a letter and its inverse.
30. `side_presentation()` returns a `Result` with the kind `InvalidPairing` (DOM-35). The alternative is an infallible function that is total on the domains that `build_with` returns, which would have to invent a presentation or panic for a domain corrupted through private access.
31. The test "Long cycle word" (DOM-36). No double that this crate builds fixes the convention of DOM-32, so the design keeps one unit test on a hexagon built through private access with a vertex cycle of three sides. The wallpaper domains have cycles of four sides, so the test also runs on cycle 1 of the built `22*`, where it asserts the reading of DOM-32 only, because every cycle of three or more letters of `**`, `xx`, `*x`, and `22*` gives the residual 0 under all three readings (DOM-36). Only the hexagon fixes the convention. The alternative is to drop the hexagon, which would leave no test that rejects the reversed base and the opposite convention.
32. The generator names `s0` upward, from the side index (`DEC-34` of the architecture). The alternative is names from 1, which would differ from the side index that the renderer records and `escher info` prints.
33. The copies of a long vertex cycle. Check 4 requires the copies of a cycle to sum to 2 (DOM-55), so a cycle of three or more vertices passes it only when a vertex has 0 copies, which does not describe its angle. The hexagon of "Long cycle word" has such a vertex (Section 12.1). The design keeps the corner with its order and copies for the doubles, because no double that this crate builds has such a cycle. The wallpaper domains, whose cycles visit up to four vertices, take the alternative: check 4 reads no copies for them, and check 5 uses the cycle rule of DOM-56, a condition on the sum of the angles of each cycle. The remaining alternative is to move the doubles to the cycle rule as well.
34. The default shape of a polygon (`DEC-13` as amended). The design takes the polygon with an inscribed circle of the proof of Theorem 7.16.2 of [BEA], with each length rounded to three decimals and clamped to the range (DOM-45). It is the regular polygon when the orders are equal. The alternative is `DEC-13` as it stood: the regular shape where the orders are equal and the length 2 otherwise, for which the research gives no formula of the regular cut lengths.
35. The parameter names (`DEC-47` of the architecture). The design takes `cornerpair<k>.length` and `slice<i>.length`, SymmHub's keys in lower case, with the corner pairs first (DOM-45). The alternatives are `slice<i>.length` for every parameter, as the plan writes for `*2223`, or `edge<k>.length` for a corner pair.
36. The limit on the corners (`DEC-48` of the architecture). The design admits four to eight corners and returns `LimitExceeded` above (DOM-38). The alternatives are a limit of 12 corners with orders up to 200, where the defaults that the prototype tried kept a margin of at least 69, or no limit below the 63 corners that `escher-signature` admits, which the prototype refutes (Section 6.12).
37. The range of the lengths (`DEC-14` as amended). The design takes the closed range 0.5 to 3.5 for both kinds and accepts that the guard refuses some settings, about 2% with seven corners and 8% with eight on the prototype (Section 9.7). The alternative is a rule on the size of the polygon, such as a bound on the normal components of its walls, in place of a box, which no prototype measured.
38. The guard (`DEC-48` of the architecture). The design runs the full verification inside `build_with` for the polygons and their doubles (DOM-44). The alternatives are to run it for every domain, which changes nothing for the other classes and doubles the cost of `escher info --check`, or to leave the verification to the caller, which `escher render` does not do.
39. The double of `p1...pn` (`DEC-50` of the architecture). The design builds it at the default shape of its starred signature with no parameter (DOM-47). The alternative is to give it the lengths of its generating polygon, which would name a partial set of the parameters of `p1...pn` and change their meaning when a later construction adds the others.
40. The refused doubles of polygons. The design evaluates check 4 of a domain with five or more vertices in double-double arithmetic (DOM-49), so the arithmetic of the check does not limit the outcome. The guard of DOM-44 refuses some doubles of polygons by check 4, at the only shape that DOM-47 gives them: in the sweeps of Section 8.4, a few doubles with eight orders of which five or six are 1000, and about one in ten of the regular doubles with eight orders, with orders from 631 to 999. It refuses no double with four to seven orders of either sweep, and no starred polygon. These refusals come from the storage of each pairing as one `f64` matrix (Section 8.4). The options are these:
    - The refusal, with `Numerical`, the text of a refused double of Section 10.2, and no tolerance raised, which is what this design does.
    - The pairing of a double stored as its two reflections, or the double built across another wall, so that check 4 reads the two reflections in place of their rounded product. This changes the stored data of the doubles, so it is a change of the design, and no prototype measured it.

    The decision is the owner's.
41. The margin at the default shape with seven or eight corners and a corner of order 1000. Over the default sweep, some of these signatures have a margin below 8 at their default shapes, and every one of them passes every check (Section 9.7). The exact polygon rounded once gives the same smallest margins, so a more accurate walk does not raise them. The design builds every polygon that the guard passes (DOM-44), claims a margin of 8 only for the orders up to 12 with four to eight corners and for a corner of order 1000 with four to six corners, and states the measured margins for the rest (Section 8.4). The alternatives are a lower order limit for seven and eight corners, a limit of six corners, side lengths of step 6 in double-double arithmetic, which would give `*(1000)2222222` the margin of about 17 of its exact lengths but would leave about as many signatures below 8 as the exact polygon rounded once has (Section 9.7), and a criterion below 8 for checks 1 and 2. The doubles of polygons with an order above 12 have margins below 8 on the measured sets from six orders on, down to just above 1 with seven and eight orders, and Section 8.4 claims a margin of 8 for the doubles with four to eight orders up to 12 only. The tests accept either outcome for a double whose smallest margin is below 2 (Section 8.4).
42. The large orders of the test "Random polygons". Every order from 900 to 1000 would weight the test toward the limit as "Random triples" does, but `escher-signature` can reject a signature of seven or eight such orders, and such a signature never reaches `build_with` (Section 12.1). The design draws the large orders from $\{900, 950, 1000\}$. The alternatives are fewer large orders in each signature, or a wider arithmetic in `escher-signature`, which its design would have to change.
43. The tile of `4*2` and `3*3`. The design takes the kite of `442` and `632` with sides 0 and 1 as mirrors, in the place of `*442` and `*632` (Section 6.15). The alternative is SymmHub's right triangle with two sides paired and the hypotenuse a mirror, which has a mirror side through a vertex and no source for `3*3`.
44. The layout of the rectangular groups. The design takes the rectangle $F$ of `*2222` with the area 1 as the tile of `**`, `xx`, `*x`, `22x`, `22*`, and `2*22`, with mirror lines and glide axes vertical (Section 6.15). The alternatives are SymmHub's horizontal layout, which `aspect` and its reciprocal reach up to a quarter turn, and tiles nested in the grid of `*2222` with the areas 2 and 4. On the prototype with this layout, the parity fill gave byte-identical images for `**` and `22*`, and for `*x`, `22x`, and `*2222`, and with one fill colour all six rectangular groups gave the image of `*2222` (Section 6.15). `docs/design/render.md` puts to the owner whether to draw the mirror sides differently (`DEC-55` of the architecture).
45. The convention of `skew` (`DEC-52` of the architecture). The design takes `skew` as the first coordinate of the second lattice vector divided by the length of the first, from $-0.5$ to $0.5$, positive when the second vector leans right, and applies it to `2222` by shearing its double, so that its numbering and its colouring ids stay (Sections 6.6 and 6.13). The alternative is a new tile for `2222`, which would renumber its sides and so rename its colouring ids (DOM-37).
46. Tests kept for a reason that does not apply. TQ-11 of `docs/design/tooling.md` puts them to the owner, including the row "Side order of `22`" of Section 12.1.
47. The trivial group `1` (`DEC-58` of the architecture). The design builds it as the whole sphere with no side, at the cost of the two changes of DOM-62, so that every valid spherical signature has a domain. The alternative is to leave `1` unsupported, which leaves `escher render 1` without an image.
48. The limit of `2*n` (`DEC-59` of the architecture). The design admits $n$ up to 500 with the text `order 501 is larger than 500` (DOM-63), because its base has the order $2n$ and the accuracy of Section 9.5 is established up to the order 1000. On the prototype, `2*(501)` to `2*(2000)` passed every check with a smallest margin of $2.7 \times 10^{3}$, and the folds converged, so accuracy did not need the limit in that range (Section 4). The alternative is a limit of 1000, which needs the range of the spherical triangles of Sections 9.5 and 16 extended to the order 2000 with new measurements, or a split triangle of `*n22` as a new construction. The decision is the owner's.
49. The lunes of order 1 (`DEC-62` of the architecture). The design builds `*` and `x` as lunes of order 1 with two sides on one great circle and exact normals (Section 6.16). The alternative is one side with no vertex, which every check and the renderer would have to accept.
50. The split mirror side of `n*` (`DEC-62` of the architecture). The design keeps the four sides of the kite of `n22`, with the equator split into two mirror sides at a flat vertex whose position `verify()` does not check (Section 6.16). The alternative is a triangle with three sides and one mirror side, whose measurement by the law of cosines breaks at $n = 2$.

### 15.4. Points Settled by Measurement

These points of the polygons rest on measurements of the crate, which this document records:

1. The method of the hyperbolic and circular functions to double-double accuracy (DOM-42). Section 6.10 states the method, after [QD], and Section 9.7 its measured accuracy. It needs no dependency, so DOM-17 stands.
2. The bounds of Section 9.7 and the residuals of the polygons and their doubles at the default shapes come from the tests of Section 12.1. Sections 8.4 and 9.7 record them, with the margins over the default sweep and the shares of refused settings.
3. The checks that fail on each defect of Section 8.3, on the built polygons at their default shapes, including the row of two adjacent corners exchanged, are those that Section 8.3 states.
4. Section 9.7 records the times of the guard in a release build and in the debug profile of the tests. Section 12.1 records the times of the polygon tests in a debug build, in the table of times after the tests of DOM-46.
5. The test "Walk against weld" is affordable. It takes 0.8 s in a debug build (Section 12.1), so `just test` runs it over the 22 named polygons at their default shapes, and Section 9.7 records its errors.

## 16. Security Considerations

The threat model is a caller that passes a `Signature` parsed from untrusted text, and settings with untrusted names and values, to `FundamentalDomain::build_with`, and then calls `verify()`. `escher-signature` has already rejected malformed text and bounded the signature (Section 16 of the signature design). The attacker can choose any valid signature. The attacker cannot change the crate, its dependencies, or the caller.

The attacks in scope are exhaustion of time or memory through a large order, a panic that stops the caller, and a wrong domain that the caller uses as correct. The crate counters them with the limit of this section, the rule that it never panics (DOM-2 and DOM-8), and the checks of Section 8.

DOM-3, DOM-18, DOM-38, and DOM-63 require the limits of this table. The input is untrusted, the cost of `verify()` grows with the order and with the number of corners, and the accuracy of Sections 9.3 and 9.7 is established only up to these limits:

| Limit | Value | Reason for the number |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Largest corner order | 1000 | The construction costs the same for every order. `verify()` costs at most about 3000 matrix products at this limit for a triangle and 8040 for a polygon of eight corners (Section 8.2), and the relator check keeps a margin of 15.0 at its tolerance of $2 \times 10^{-6}$ over every supported triple (Section 8.4). The plan tests up to this order. The number is a choice of this design (Section 15.3). A higher limit needs a construction of the far walls that is better conditioned, which the research does not source, and new measurements of every margin |
| `aspect` | 0.125 to 8, inclusive | A finite value in a bounded range keeps the rectangle within the scale that the tests of Section 9.4 measure. NaN and infinities are rejected (DOM-18). The range is a choice of this design (Section 15.3) |
| `skew` | $-0.5$ to 0.5, inclusive | The range reaches every lattice of the family (Section 6.6), and a finite value keeps the domain within the grid that Sections 8.4 and 9.8 measure. NaN and infinities are rejected (DOM-18). The range is a choice of this design (item 45 of Section 15.3) |
| Order $n$ of `2*n` | 500 | DOM-63. The base of `2*n` has a corner of the order $2n$, which stays within the largest corner order above. The number is a choice of this design (item 48 of Section 15.3) |
| Number of orders | 8 | DOM-38. Beyond it the margins of the checks can fall below 8 even for an exact construction, and the cost of the construction, of `verify()`, and of the colouring search grows with the corners (Section 6.12). The number is a choice of this design (Section 15.3) |
| `cornerpair<k>.length` and `slice<i>.length` | 0.5 to 3.5, inclusive | A finite value in a bounded range keeps the walls of a polygon close enough to the centre for the guard to pass most settings (Section 9.7). NaN and infinities are rejected (DOM-18). The range is a choice of this design (Section 15.3) |

The limit is a private constant, and unit tests inside the crate read it. It applies in the same way to the orders of `*n22` and `*nn` and of every rotation signature, and `verify()` of a spherical domain or of a double costs about as many products as at the hyperbolic limit (Section 8.2). `build_with` allocates at most 14 sides and 14 vertices, for the double of a polygon with eight corners, and at most 6 sheets and 18 gaps for a polygon, so the memory of a domain does not depend on the input. The bisection of DOM-45 stops after at most 200 steps, so a default cannot loop. The walk of check 4 stops after $2m$ steps for each cycle, so a corrupted partner cannot make it loop. A setting is only compared with the names of the `ParameterSpec` values and stored, and the error text echoes no setting (Section 10.2), so the number and the length of the settings need no limit.

For a domain that `build_with` returns, `side_presentation()` builds at most 14 generators and 16 relators, each base of at most six letters, with an exponent of at most 1000, so its memory does not depend on the input beyond the order limit. A domain with more than 256 sides, which only private access builds, gets `InvalidPairing` before any relator is built. The walk of the cycles is the one of check 4 and stops after $2m$ steps for each cycle. The function runs no coset enumeration. The cost of an enumeration of the presentation, and its limits, belong to `escher-group` (Section 15 of the group design).

The other topics of Section 3.2 of `docs/writing-documentation.md` apply as follows:

- Unsafe code: the crate has none, because the workspace denies `unsafe_code`, and it calls no native library.
- Untrusted input and file paths: the signature is the only input. The crate reads and writes no file and keeps no state between calls.
- Build scripts: the crate has none. The procedural macro of `thiserror` runs at build time on the source of this crate only.
- Dependencies: `Cargo.lock` fixes every version, and cargo-deny checks licences, advisories, duplicates, and sources under the policy of Section 8 of `docs/design/tooling.md`, for the dev-dependencies as well as the normal ones.

Attacks on the build machine, the repository, or the caller are out of scope, because this crate neither builds nor prints anything, and the command-line design governs what `escher-cli` prints.

A polygon built from untrusted lengths is verified before it is returned (DOM-44), so a wrong polygon from an extreme setting reaches no caller. The same holds for the ten domains of Section 6.13 with untrusted values of `aspect` and `skew` (DOM-54), whose guard costs a few dozen matrix products, and for the spherical group domains other than that of `*` (DOM-60), whose guard costs at most about 3000 matrix products at the limits. The cost of the guard is one call of `verify()`, at most 8040 matrix products (Section 8.2).

The residual risk is a domain that is wrong by less than the tolerances. A defect that moves a wall or an angle by less than $10^{-9}$, or a relator by less than $2 \times 10^{-6}$ after the division by its order, passes every check. A second residual risk is in the measurements. The sweeps of Sections 8.4 and 9.3 run outside the repository, and no test repeats them. The committed tests measure every triple at which the sweeps have a largest value, but no measurement covers the vertices, normals, and side lengths of every supported triple (Section 9.3), and Section 14 lists the figures that no measurement of the code covers.

## 17. Operational Considerations

The crate declares no Cargo feature, reads no environment variable, and has no build script. It is pure Rust with no platform-specific code, and the supported platform of the proof of concept is Linux (Section 12 of the architecture). The MSRV is the one that the workspace declares in `rust-version`. The crate emits no log.

Every failure of `build` is an `Error` with an `ErrorKind`. A failed check is not an error. `verify()` reports it in the `Verification`, and the caller decides what a failed check means:

| Failure | Reported as | Recovery |
| --- | --- | --- |
| A valid signature without a construction | `UnsupportedSignature` | None in this crate. A general non-compact signature has none in the proof of concept (`DEC-19`) |
| More than eight orders | `LimitExceeded` | Use at most eight corners or cone points |
| A corner order above 1000 | `LimitExceeded` | Use orders of at most 1000 |
| The order $n$ of `2*n` above 500 | `LimitExceeded` | Use $n$ of at most 500 |
| A spherical group domain that fails a check of its guard | `Numerical` | None. No order in range is known to produce it, and one that does is a defect of the crate |
| A polygon or its double that fails a check of its guard | `Numerical` | Choose lengths nearer the defaults that `parameters` lists |
| One of the ten domains of Section 6.13 that fails a check of its guard | `Numerical` | None. No setting in range is known to produce it, and one that does is a defect of the crate |
| A setting whose name is not a parameter of the signature | `UnknownParameter` | Use a name that `parameters` lists |
| A value that is not finite or is outside the range of its parameter | `ParameterOutOfRange` | Use a value within the range that `parameters` lists |
| A degenerate construction | `Numerical` | None. No supported signature is known to produce it outside the guard, and one that does is a defect of the crate |
| A domain without a side presentation | `InvalidPairing`, from `side_presentation()` | None. No domain that `build_with` returns produces it, and one that does is a defect of the crate |
| A check outside its tolerance | `Check::passed` is false | None. It is a defect of the crate, and the residual and the tolerance locate it |

| DOM-3 | `build` returns `LimitExceeded` for a supported signature with an order above 1000, after the support check, and `2*n` takes the limit of DOM-63 instead | 4 |

| DOM-5 | `build_with` constructs a hyperbolic reflection triangle by the steps of Section 6.1, a polygon by those of Section 6.10, a Euclidean domain by those of Section 6.5, a spherical one by those of Section 6.7, a double by those of Section 6.8, a wallpaper domain and the shear of `2222` by those of Section 6.13, and a spherical group domain by those of Section 6.16, in order | 6.1 |

| DOM-18 | `build_with` checks support, the limits on the number of orders and on the order, the names, and the values, in this order, and returns the first error that applies | 6.6 |
| DOM-19 | A test of a bound names its input set and prints its largest value, and this document records the bound only where it is part of a contract | 9.4 |
| DOM-20 | The angle of a domain with two vertices is measured from its two normals, by the clamped arccosine for a lune, which includes the domains of `nx`, `x`, and `*`, and by `atan2` for the double of a lune, and no other angle is | 7 |
| DOM-21 | For a spherical domain, the vertex precondition of checks 2 to 6 is finite and not the zero vector, with no condition on the last coordinate | 8.2 |
| DOM-22 | The exhaustive certificates cover every lune of their sets and stay while `UNV-29` is open | 12.1 |
| DOM-23 | A rotation signature is built as the double of the reflection domain of its starred signature across the wall of side 1, with that domain left in place and its centre as the centre, except that `2222` with `skew` other than 0 is the shear of that double | 6.8 |
| DOM-24 | Vertices, sides, and partners of a double follow Section 6.8, with the partner $(2n - 1 - k) \bmod (2n - 2)$ of side $k$ | 6.8 |
| DOM-25 | The pairing of an original side is $R_1 R_j$ and of an image side $R_j R_1$, each the compensated product of the two stored reflections, entry by entry | 6.8 |
| DOM-26 | Two halves on one wall at a corner of order 2, or at the centre of a half-turn of a wallpaper domain, stay two sides that meet at a flat vertex, and no paired side is its own partner | 6.8 |
| DOM-27 | Check 2 fails unless the kind, the partner, the orientation, the pairing, and the geometry of every side agree, with `GlideReflection` and `RotaryReflection` reversing and paired with another side, `RotaryReflection` on a spherical domain only and `GlideReflection` off it, and the kind `Translation` exactly for a pairing that passes the translation test, which for a hyperbolic pairing requires a positive determinant and a trace above 3 | 8.2 |
| DOM-28 | Check 4 of a domain with a side that is not a mirror walks the vertex cycles by DOM-55 and evaluates each cycle relator, and a domain whose sides are all mirrors evaluates its corner relators | 8.2 |
| DOM-29 | The angles and the area of a hyperbolic or spherical kite are measured from its two triangles across the doubled wall | 7 |
| DOM-30 | The stored values and the check residuals of every reflection domain do not depend on the code of the doubles, the polygons, the wallpaper domains, and the spherical group domains | 8.4 |
| DOM-31 | The exhaustive certificates cover the spherical doubles and stay while `UNV-22` or `UNV-30` is open | 12.1 |
| DOM-32 | `side_presentation()` gives one generator for each side, named `s<i>`, for $P_i^{-1}$, with words read left to right as products | 5.4 |
| DOM-33 | A reflection domain gives the squares $s_j^2$ and then the corner relators $(s_j s_{j+1})^{n_j}$, in that order | 5.4 |
| DOM-34 | A domain without mirrors gives the pair relators and then the cycle relators of the walk of DOM-55, with each base in walk order and the order of the cycle as exponent | 5.4 |
| DOM-35 | A domain with a number of corners other than its number of sides, more than 256 sides, a walk that does not close within $2m$ steps, a cycle with unequal orders or, for a double, copies that do not sum to 2, or a relator or presentation that `escher-group` rejects gives `InvalidPairing`, checked in that order, without a panic | 5.4 |
| DOM-36 | "Relators evaluate" covers every domain of its sets and "Long cycle word" exists, and both stay while `UNV-32` is open | 12.1 |
| DOM-37 | The numbering of the sides of a built domain is part of the public contract, and depends on no setting, because the colouring ids depend on it | 5.4 |
| DOM-38 | `build` returns `LimitExceeded` for a supported signature with more than eight orders, after the predicate and before the order limit | 4 |
| DOM-39 | The features, cuts, and sheets of a polygon come from the canonical corner orders, each cut sheet is recorded as $(y, x, \bar s_i)$, and a gap that breaks the contiguity condition gives `Numerical` | 6.10 |
| DOM-40 | The features of a sheet keep their counterclockwise cyclic order and are never exchanged | 6.10 |
| DOM-41 | Every gap of every sheet comes from the one gap function with its two branches, and a side is its corner-pair value or the sum of its gaps | 6.10 |
| DOM-42 | The walk and the placement of a polygon run in double-double arithmetic, with each vertex rounded to `f64` once after the placement | 6.10 |
